Mr. Stott kept hanging to his bed-rail, a heavily loaded cane. He had no intention of going nearer to Mayfield than the safe side of his dining-room window, but the holding of the stick gave him the self-confidence of which he was in need.

Cautiously the girl let up the blind of the dining-room window and unfastened the catch. The sash slid up noiselessly and gave them an interrupted view of Mayfield.

===Chinese===

“怎么回事，艾琳——一大早这个钟点把我吵醒？”斯托特先生气恼地问。

“他们在那儿——那些中国人。我看见有一个正从窗户往里钻，”姑娘说，牙齿咯咯打颤，害得比尔伯里医生的“快治痛”大受其扰。

“稍等，我去拿手杖。”

斯托特先生的床栏上常年挂着一条沉甸甸的灌铅手杖。他压根不打算走近梅菲尔德一步——最多只到自家饭厅窗户安全的一侧——可手里握着手杖，便给了他正需要的那份胆气。

姑娘小心翼翼地卷起饭厅窗上的百叶帘，拨开窗闩。窗扇无声地滑上去，给了他们一幅半遮半掩望向梅菲尔德的视野。

===Original===

“There’s one!” whispered Eline.

Standing in the shadow was a figure. Mr. Stott saw it plainly. They watched in silence for the greater part of half-an-hour. Mr. Stott had an idea that he ought to telephone for the police, but refrained. In the case of ordinary burglars, he would not have hesitated. But these were Chinese, notoriously clannish and vengeful. He had read stories, in which Chinamen had inflicted diabolical injuries upon men who had betrayed them.

At the end of the half-hour’s vigil, the door of Mayfield opened and a man came out and joined the other. Together they walked up the road and that was the last Mr. Stott saw of them.

“Very remarkable!” said Mr. Stott profoundly. “I’m glad you called me, Eline. I wouldn’t have missed this for the world. But you must say nothing about this, Eline—nothing. The Chinese people are very bloodthirsty. They would think no more of putting you into a barrel full of sharp pointed nails and rolling you down a hill, than I should think of—er—lacing my shoes.”

So Maple Manor kept its grisly secret and none knew of Yeh Ling’s visit to the house of death or his search for the tiny lacquer box wherein Jesse Trasmere kept a folded sheet of thin paper elegantly inscribed in Chinese characters by Yeh Ling, in his own hand.

===Chinese===

“有一个！”艾琳悄声道。

暗影里立着一个人影。斯托特先生看得一清二楚。两人一声不响地盯了将近半个钟头。斯托特先生想到应该打电话叫警察，但还是忍住了。若是寻常的窃贼，他不会犹豫。可这些是中国人，出了名的抱团和记仇。他读过一些故事，说中国人怎样对出卖他们的人施加恶魔般的毒手。

这场半个钟头的守望到了头，梅菲尔德的门开了，出来一个男人，同另一个会合。两人一道沿街走去——这是斯托特先生最后一回见到他们。

“真是了不得！”斯托特先生意味深长地说，“亏得你叫醒我，艾琳。这一幕说什么我也不能错过。可这事你一个字也不许提，艾琳——一个字也不许。中国人残忍嗜杀得很。把你塞进一只钉满尖钉的木桶里推下山坡，在他们眼里，就跟我要——呃——要系一下鞋带一样算不得什么。”

就这样，枫园（Maple Manor）守住了它那阴森的秘密：谁也不知道叶凌曾造访这座死亡之屋，谁也不知道他曾搜寻那只小小的漆盒——杰西·特拉斯米尔在盒中收着一张折起的薄纸，纸上雅致的汉字，正是叶凌亲笔所书。

## XII / 十二

===Original===
“Ursula Ardfern is leaving the stage and is going to live in the country.”

Tab made the announcement one evening when he came home from the office.

Rex scarcely seemed interested.

“Oh?” said Rex.

That was all he said. He seemed as disinclined as Tab to discuss the lady.

===Chinese===
“乌苏拉·阿德芬要退出舞台，到乡下定居去了。”

一天晚上，塔布从报馆下班回来，宣布了这条消息。

雷克斯显得兴致寥寥。

“哦？”雷克斯说。

他就说了这么一句。同塔布一样，他似乎也不愿谈论这位女士。

===Original===
It was his last night at the Doughty Street flat. He was still suffering from shock, and his doctor had advised a trip abroad. He had suggested that at the end of his vacation he would return to Doughty Street, but on this point Tab was firm.

“You have a lot of money, Babe,” he said seriously, “and a man who has a lot of money has also a whole lot of responsibilities. There are about a hundred and forty-five reasons why our little ménage should be broken up, and the most important from my point of view is, that I will not be demoralized by living cheek by jowl with a man of millions. You have a certain place to take in society, certain duties to perform, and you can’t keep up the position that you are entitled to keep, in a half-flat in Doughty Street. I don’t suppose you ever want to go to Mayfield to live.”

Rex shuddered.

===Chinese===
这是他住在道蒂街公寓的最后一夜。他惊魂未定，医生劝他出国游历一趟。他提议假期结束后仍回道蒂街来住，可在这一点上塔布寸步不让。

“你如今有的是钱，贝比。”他一本正经地说，“而钱多的人，责任也就一大堆。咱们这个搭伙的小家该散伙，理由少说也有一百四十五条；依我看最要紧的一条是：我不肯跟一位百万富翁脸贴脸地同住，把自己住得没了志气。你在社会上该占一席之地，该尽一定的义务；守着道蒂街半套公寓，你维持不起你理应享有的体面。我料想你总不至于想搬去梅菲尔德住吧。”

雷克斯打了个寒噤。

===Original===
“I don’t,” he said with great earnestness. “I shall shut the place up and let it stand for a few years, until the memory of the crime is forgotten, and then perhaps somebody will buy it. I am pretty comfortable here, Tab.”

“I am not thinking so much about your comfort as my own,” replied Tab calmly. “It isn’t going to do me a lot of good in any way. Consider yourself ejected.”

Rex grinned.

===Chinese===
“我才不想呢。”他说得极其恳切，“我要把那宅子封起来，让它空上几年，等那桩罪案淡出人们的记忆，兴许会有谁来买下它。我在这儿过得挺舒服，塔布。”

“我惦记的倒不是你的舒坦，是我自己的。”塔布不动声色地答道，“无论如何，你留下来对我没多少好处。你就当自己已经被扫地出门了吧。”

雷克斯咧嘴一笑。

===Original===
He sailed for Naples the next afternoon, and Tab went down to the boat to see him off. No mention of Ursula Ardfern was made until the landing bell was ringing.

“I am holding you to your promise, Tab, to introduce me to Miss Ardfern,” he said, and frowned as though at some unhappy recollection. “I wish to heaven she hadn’t been mixed up in the business at all. How on earth do you account for her jewel-case being in poor Uncle Jesse’s vault? By-the-way, the key of that devil room is in my trunk if the police want it. I don’t suppose they will, for they have the other key now.”

He had asked this question about Ursula’s jewels so many times before, that Tab could not keep count of them. Therefore, he did not attempt to supply a satisfactory solution.

===Chinese===
第二天下午，他登船去那不勒斯（Naples），塔布到码头送行。直到登船铃响起，才有人提起乌苏拉·阿德芬。

“塔布，你答应过引见我认识阿德芬小姐，这诺言我可要你兑现的。”他说着皱起眉头，像是想起什么不愉快的旧事。“老天作证，她压根儿就不该被卷进这桩事里来。她的珠宝匣究竟怎么会跑到可怜的杰西叔叔的保险库里，你到底怎么解释？对了，那间鬼屋的钥匙在我的皮箱里，警察要就给他们。我看不会，因为另一把钥匙如今在他们手里了。”

关于乌苏拉的珠宝，这个问题他从前已问过不知多少遍，塔布早已数不清了。因此，这一回他也不打算给出一个叫人满意的解答。

===Original===
Standing on the pier he watched the big ship gliding down the river, and on the whole was glad that the companionship had broken up. He liked Rex and Rex liked him, and they had shared happily the mild vicissitudes which came to young men with large ambitions and limited incomes. Of the two, Tab had been the richer in the old days, and had often helped the other through the morasses which grip the ankles of men who systematically live beyond their means. And now Babe was in calm waters: forevermore superior to the favours of crabbed uncles and businesslike employers: no more would he start at every knock the postman rapped, or scowl at the letters which arrived, knowing that more than half of them were bills he could not hope to satisfy.

Nearly a month had passed since the inquest, and all that Tab had heard about Ursula was that she had been very ill and was now in the country, presumably at the Stone Cottage. He had some idea of going down to see her, but thought better of it.

Meanwhile, he had made respectful enquiries about the girl who had so impressed him.

===Chinese===
他站在码头上，望着大船顺流徐徐滑去，心里总的说是高兴的——这段同居之谊就此散了。他喜欢雷克斯，雷克斯也喜欢他，两人曾快快活活地一同挨过那些志向远大而进项有限的年轻人难免的浮沉。想当年，两人里塔布手头宽裕些，屡屡搭救同伴，把他从那专咬入不敷出成性之人脚踝的泥沼里拽出来。如今贝比驶进了风平浪静的水域：从此再不必仰承乖僻叔叔和公事公办的雇主们的恩典；再不会邮差每敲一下门便心里一惊，也不会对着寄到的信件皱眉苦脸——他明知其中一多半是账单，是他无从指望偿清的。

验尸审讯以来已过去将近一个月。关于乌苏拉，塔布听到的全部消息，只是她大病了一场，如今在乡下，多半就在石屋。他起过下乡探望的念头，转念又作罢了。

这期间，他对这位令他印象至深的姑娘做了一番恭敬的打听。

===Original===
Ursula Ardfern’s story was a curious one. She had appeared first in a road company, playing small parts and playing them well. Then without any warning, she blossomed forth into management, took a lease of the Athenaeum and appeared playing a secondary role in an adaptation of *Tosca*﻿—the lead being in the capable hands of Mary Farrelli. The dramatic critics were mollified by her modesty and pleased with her acting: said they would like to see her in a more important part, and hoped that her season would be prosperous. They asked, amongst themselves, who was the man behind the show and found no satisfactory answer. When *Tosca* came off, after a run of three months, she staged *The Tremendous Jones*, which played for a year, and this time she was the leading actress. She had gone from success to success, was on the very threshold of a great career. The simple announcement that she had retired from the stage forever was not very seriously believed. Yet it was true. Ursula Ardfern had appeared for the last time before the footlights.

The day that Rex sailed she saved Tab any further cogitation by writing to him. He found the letter at the office.

> “Dear Mr. Holland: I wonder if you would come to Stone Cottage to see me? I promise you rather a sensational ‘story,’ though I realize that it will lose much of its importance because I will not have my name mentioned in connection.”

===Chinese===
乌苏拉·阿德芬的经历说来蹊跷。她最初在一家巡回剧团里露面，演些小角色，而且演得都不坏。后来毫无征兆，她忽然大放异彩，自己当起班主来：租下雅典娜剧院（Athenaeum），在《托斯卡》（Tosca）的改编版里出演一个次要角色——主角由身手不凡的玛丽·法雷利（Mary Farrelli）担纲。剧评家们被她的谦逊抚平了挑剔，又对她的演技感到满意，说愿看她演更重要的角色，盼她这一演出季顺遂兴旺。他们私下互相打听：这台戏幕后出钱的男人是谁？却没打听出一个所以然来。《托斯卡》连演三个月下档后，她又推出《了不起的琼斯》（The Tremendous Jones），一连演了一年，这一回她是领衔女主角了。她一路成功接着成功，正站在一项伟大事业的门槛上。因此，她永远退出舞台的那纸简短声明，起初没有多少人当真。可这是真的。乌苏拉·阿德芬已在脚灯前做了最后一次亮相。

雷克斯启程那天，她来了信，替塔布省去了进一步的苦苦思量。这封信他是在报馆见到的。

> “亲爱的荷兰先生：不知您可愿来石屋见我一面？我答应给您一个相当轰动的‘故事’，虽然我也明白，因为不肯让我的名字与之相联，它要失色不少。”

===Original===
Tab would have liked to have gone then and there. He was up the next morning at six, and chafed because he could not in decency arrive at the house much before lunch.

It was a glorious June day, warm, with a gentle westerly wind, such a day as every doctor with a convalescent patient in his charge, hails with joy and thankfulness.

She was reclining where he had seen her on his first visit to Hertford, but this time she did not rise, but held out a thin, white hand, which he took with such exaggerated care that she laughed. She was paler, thinner of face, older looking in some indefinite way.

===Chinese===
塔布恨不得当场就动身。第二天早晨他六点便起了床，随后又焦躁不已——碍着体面，总不能赶在午饭前许久便闯上门去。

那是个明媚的六月天，暖融融的，拂着轻柔的西风——这样的日子，每一位看护着康复期病人的医生见了，都要欢欣感激。

她斜倚在他头一次到赫特福德拜访时见到她的那个位置，这一回却没有起身，只伸出一只纤细白皙的手。他握得那样小心翼翼、近乎夸张，惹得她笑了。她脸色更白了，面颊清减了，眉眼间说不清道不明地显出老态。

===Original===
“You won’t break it,” she said. “Sit down, Mr. Tab.”

“I like Mr. Tab very much better than I like Mr. Holland,” said Tab. “It is glorious here. Why do we swelter in the towns?”

“Because the towns pay us our salaries,” she said drily. “Mr. Holland, will you do something for me?”

===Chinese===
“你握不坏它的。”她说，“坐吧，塔布先生。”

“比起荷兰先生，我可喜欢塔布先生多了。”塔布说，“这儿真是好风光。咱们何苦在城里活受蒸笼罪？”

“因为薪水是城里发给咱们的。”她干巴巴地说，“荷兰先生，你肯替我办件事吗？”

===Original===
He longed to tell her that if she asked him to stand on his head, or lie down whilst she wiped her feet upon him, she would be gladly obeyed. Instead:

“Why, of course,” he said.

“Will you sell some jewels for me? They are those which were found﻿—in poor Mr. Trasmere’s vault.”

===Chinese===
他真想告诉她：哪怕她叫他倒立，或者叫他躺下、由她在他身上蹭脚，他也会欢天喜地照办。可他嘴里说的是：

“咳，那还用说。”他说。

“你肯替我把一些珠宝卖掉吗？就是那批在可怜的特拉斯米尔先生保险库里发现的。”

===Original===
“Sell your jewels,” he said in amazement, “why? Are you﻿—” he checked himself.

“I am not very poor,” she said quietly, “I have enough money to live on without working again﻿—my last play was a very great success, and happily the profits﻿—” She stopped dead. “At any rate, I am not poor.”

“Then why sell your jewelry? Are you going to buy others?” he blurted out.

She shook her head, and a smile dawned in her eyes.

===Chinese===
“卖掉你的珠宝？”他愕然道，“为什么？你是不是——”他把话咽了回去。

“我并不很穷。”她平静地说，“我的钱够我此生不再工作也过活得下去——我的最后一出戏大获成功，而且幸好，赚头——”她猛地收住。“反正，我不穷。”

“那你干吗还要卖首饰？打算另买新的吗？”他脱口而出。

她摇了摇头，眼里绽出一丝笑意。

===Original===
“No, my plan is this: I am going to sell the jewelry for what it is worth, and then I want you to distribute the money to such charities as you think best.”

He was too astonished to answer, and she went on:

“I know very little about charities and their values. I know in some cases all the money subscribed is swallowed up in officials’ salaries. But you will know these.”

“Are you serious?” he at last found his voice to ask.

===Chinese===
“不，我的打算是这样：把这些首饰按实价卖掉，然后请你把钱分给你认为最合适的慈善机构。”

他惊讶得答不上话来，她接着说：

“我对慈善机构和它们的底细所知甚少。我知道有些地方，捐来的钱统统叫职员们的薪水吞了个精光。这些你是内行。”

“你是当真的？”他总算找回声音，问道。

===Original===
“Quite,” she nodded gravely. “I think they are worth from twelve to twenty thousand. I am not sure. They are mine,” she went on a little defiantly, and unnecessarily so, thought Tab, “and I may do as I wish with them. I want them to be sold and the money distributed.”

“But my dear Miss Ardfern﻿—” he began.

“My dear Mr. Holland!” she mocked him, “you must do as I tell you if you are going to help me at all.”

“I’ll certainly carry out your wishes,” he said, “but it is a weighty lot of money to give away.”

===Chinese===
“千真万确。”她郑重地点点头。“我想它们值一万二到两万英镑，我说不准。它们是我的。”她略带挑衅地往下说——塔布心想，这挑衅全无必要，“我高兴怎么处置就怎么处置。我要把它们卖掉，把钱散出去。”

“可是，亲爱的阿德芬小姐——”他开口道。

“亲爱的荷兰先生！”她学着他的腔调打趣道，“你要帮我这个忙，就得听我的。”

“你的意思我一定照办。”他说，“只是这么一大笔钱送出去，分量不轻啊。”

===Original===
“It is a weighter lot of money to keep,” she said quietly. “There is another favour I ask﻿—you must not write that I am the donor. You can describe me as a society woman, a retired trades-woman, or as anything you like, except as an actress, and of course my name must not even be hinted. Will you do this?”

He nodded.

“I have them here,” she said. “I kept them at the hotel and had them sent down to me by special messenger yesterday. And now that that business is over, come inside and lunch.”

===Chinese===
“留着这笔钱，分量才更重呢。”她平静地说。“我还求你另一件事——你千万不能写明捐助人是我。你可以把我描绘成一位交际名媛、一位退了业的女商人，随你怎么说都行，就是不能说我是女演员；当然，我的名字更是连点一下都不能。这个你肯做吗？”

他点了点头。

“东西就在我这里。”她说，“我一直把它们存在饭店里，昨天才叫专差给我送下乡来。好了，这桩事算是办完了，进屋去吃午饭吧。”

===Original===
It was very dear to have her leaning on his arm; her dependence thrilled him. He wanted to take her up in his arms and carry her through that sweet-smelling place, slowly and with dignity, as nurses carry sleeping babies. He wondered what she would think and say, if she guessed his thoughts. It made him hot to consider the possibility for a second.

She did not go direct to the house, but took him through a sunken patch hidden by low bushes, and he stopped and admired, for here a master hand had laid out a Chinese garden with tiny bridges and dwarf trees and great clumps of waxen rock flowers that harboured a faint and delicate scent, a hint of which came up to him.

“You were thinking of carrying me,” she said, apropros of nothing.

===Chinese===
她倚着他的手臂，这份亲昵说不出的珍贵；她的倚赖叫他心头发热。他真想把她拦腰抱起，抱着她穿过那片香风阵阵的园子，慢慢地、郑重地，像保姆抱着熟睡的婴儿。他暗自琢磨，倘若她猜中了他的念头，会怎么想、怎么说？这可能性只消想上一秒，已叫他脸上发烧。

她没有径直进屋，而是领着他穿过一片矮灌木掩映的洼地。他驻足赞叹——这里有一位高手布置起一座中国园林：小小的桥、盆景般的矮树，还有一大丛一大丛蜡质的岩花，蕴着一缕清幽淡雅的香气，丝丝缕缕沁上他的鼻端。

“你刚才想着要抱我走呢。”她没头没脑地说了一句。

===Original===
Tab went a fiery red.

“But for the proprieties, I should like it. Do you like babies, Mr. Tab?”

“I love ’em,” said he, glad to reach a less embarrassing topic.

===Chinese===
塔布的脸腾地红到了耳根。

“要不是碍着体统，我倒乐意之至。你喜欢小娃娃吗，塔布先生？”

“喜欢得很。”他说，庆幸总算换了个不那么难为情的话题。

===Original===
“So do I﻿—I have seen so many when I was a child. They are wonderful. It seems to me that they are so near to the source of life they bring with them the very fragrance of God.”

He was silent, impressed, a little bewildered. Where had she seen “so many” babies? Had she been a nurse? She had not been talking for effect.﻿ ﻿… He knew an actress once, the only other one he had interviewed, who had quoted Ovid and Herrick and had talked with astonishing ease and fluency on the Byzantine Empire. He learnt from a friend that she possessed an extraordinary memory and had read up these subjects before he came, in order to get a good story about herself. She had the story.

No, Ursula was different. He wished he had lifted her up in his arms when she had spoken about being carried.

===Chinese===
“我也喜欢——小时候我见过好多好多。娃娃们真是奇妙。在我看，他们离生命的源头那么近，简直随身带着上帝的芬芳。”

他沉默了，心里受了触动，又有点茫然。她是在哪儿见过“好多好多”娃娃的？她当过保姆不成？她这番话可不是说给人听的效果。……他从前认识一个女演员——那是他采访过的唯一另一位——张口奥维德（Ovid），闭口赫里克（Herrick），谈起拜占庭帝国来流利从容得叫人咋舌。后来他从一位朋友处得知，那人记性惊人，在他登门之前专门啃过这些题目，好让自己身上出一个好故事。故事也确实让她得着了。

不，乌苏拉不一样。他懊悔方才她说起被人抱的时候，自己没有当真把她抱起来。

===Original===
Over the meal the talk took a personal turn.

“Have you many friends?” she asked.

“Only one,” smiled Tab, “and he’s now so rich that I can scarcely call him a friend. Not that Rex wouldn’t repudiate that.”

“Rex?”

===Chinese===
席间，谈话转到了私人话题上。

“你朋友多吗？”她问。

“只有一个。”塔布笑道，“如今他阔得让我几乎不好意思再叫他朋友了。倒不是雷克斯会不认这份交情。”

“雷克斯？”

===Original===
“Rex Lander,” said Tab, “who by-the-way, is very anxious to be introduced to you. He is one of your most fervent admirers.” Tab felt that he was being very noble indeed, and he experienced quite a virtuous glow at his own unselfishness.

“Who is he?” she asked.

“He is old Trasmere’s nephew.”

“Why, of course,” she said quickly, and went red. “You have spoken about him before.”

===Chinese===
“雷克斯·兰德。”塔布说，“顺带一说，他正巴望着有人引见给你呢。他是你最热切的仰慕者之一。”塔布觉得自己这会儿实在高尚得很，心里为这份无私泛起一阵道义的暖意。

“他是谁？”她问。

“他是老特拉斯米尔的侄子。”

“啊，对了。”她飞快地说，脸红了。“你从前说起过他。”

===Original===
Tab tried to remember. He was almost certain that he had never mentioned Rex to the girl.

“So he is very rich? Of course, he would be. He was Mr. Trasmere’s only nephew.”

“You saw that in the newspaper?”

“No, I guessed, or somebody told me; I haven’t read any account of the murder, or any of the proceedings. I was too ill. He must be very rich,” she went on. “Is he anything like his uncle?”

===Chinese===
塔布努力回想。他几乎可以肯定，自己从不曾向这位姑娘提起过雷克斯。

“那么说他很有钱了？当然，他自然会的。他是特拉斯米尔先生唯一的侄子嘛。”

“这是你在报纸上看来的？”

“不，是我猜的，要不就是听谁说起过；凶案的报道我一篇也没读过，各种审讯程序也一概没看。我病得太重了。他一定非常有钱。”她接着说，“他长得像他叔叔吗？”

===Original===
Tab smiled.

“I can’t imagine two people more dissimilar,” he said. “Rex is﻿—well, he’s rather stoutish,” he said loyally, “and a lazy old horse. Mr. Trasmere, on the contrary, was very thin, and, for his age, remarkably energetic. When did I mention Rex?” he asked.

She shook her head.

“I can’t recall the time and place. Please don’t make me think, Mr. Holland. Where is Rex now?”

===Chinese===
塔布笑了。

“我想象不出还有哪两个人比他们更不相似。”他说，“雷克斯么——呃，他块头相当富态。”他忠心耿耿地补了一句，“而且是匹懒老马。特拉斯米尔先生呢，恰恰相反，瘦得很，而且以他的年纪而论，精力旺盛得惊人。我是什么时候提起过雷克斯来着？”他问。

她摇了摇头。

“时间地点我记不得了。请别逼我想了，荷兰先生。雷克斯眼下在哪儿？”

===Original===
“He has gone to Italy. He sailed yesterday,” said Tab, and thereupon the girl’s interest in Rex Lander seemed to suffer eclipse.

“I should like to have had Trasmere’s real story,” said Tab, “he must have lived an interesting life. It is rather curious that we found nothing in the house reminiscent of his Chinese experience but a small lacquer box, which was empty. The Chinese fascinate me.”

“Do they?” she looked at him quickly, “they fascinate me in a way by their kindness.”

“You know them; have you lived in China?”

===Chinese===
“他去意大利了。昨天上的船。”塔布说。话音落处，姑娘对雷克斯·兰德的兴趣似乎顿时黯淡了下去。

“我倒真想听听特拉斯米尔的真实经历。”塔布说，“他这一生想必过得很精彩。说来奇怪，我们在宅子里竟找不到一件让人想起他中国岁月的东西，只有一只小漆盒，还是空的。中国人叫我着迷。”

“是吗？”她飞快地看了他一眼，“叫我着迷的，在某种意义上是他们的善良。”

“你认识中国人？你在中国住过？”

===Original===
She shook her head.

“I know one or two,” she said, and paused as though she were considering whether it was advisable to say any more. “When I first came to town from service﻿—”

He gaped at her.

“I don’t quite get that﻿—by ‘service’ what do you mean? You don’t mean domestic service﻿—you weren’t a cook or anything?” he asked jocularly, and to his amazement she nodded.

===Chinese===
她摇了摇头。

“我认识一两个。”她说，顿了顿，像是在掂量再说下去是否妥当。“我当初从帮佣人家进城的时候——”

他目瞪口呆地望着她。

“这个我可有点儿不懂——你说‘帮佣’是什么意思？你总不会是说当女仆吧——你不是当过厨娘什么的吧？”他打趣地问。不料她竟然点头，叫他大吃一惊。

===Original===
“I was a sort of tweeny maid: peeled potatoes and washed dishes,” she said calmly, “I was only thirteen at the time. But that is another story, as Mr. Kipling says. At this age, and before I went to school, I met a Chinaman whose son was very ill. He lodged in the house where I was staying. The landlady wasn’t a very humane sort of person, and being Chinese, she thought the poor little boy had some mysterious Eastern disease which she would ‘catch.’ I nursed him, in a way,” she said apologetically, but Tab knew that the apology was not for her condescension, but for her lack of nursing skill. “The father was very poor then, a waiter in a native restaurant, but he was ever so grateful. Quite an extraordinary man﻿—I have seen him since.”

“And the child?”

===Chinese===
“我算是个打杂的小丫头：削土豆、洗碗碟。”她平静地说，“那时我才十三岁。不过那是另一个故事了，正如吉卜林先生（Kipling）常说的。就在那个年纪、还没上学的时候，我认识了一个中国人，他的儿子病得很重。那人寄住在我帮佣的人家。房东太太不是个多厚道的人，又因为这小男孩是中国人，便认定这可怜的孩子得了什么神秘的东方病，生怕会‘过’给她似的。我呢，也算是看护过他。”她说得带着歉意，可塔布明白，这歉意并非为自己屈尊俯就，而是为自己看护手艺的欠缺。“那位父亲那时穷得很，在一家本地馆子当侍者，可他感激得不得了。是个相当不寻常的人——后来我还见过他。”

“那孩子呢？”

===Original===
“Oh, he got better﻿—his father was dosing him with quaint proprietary medicines. I think he was suffering from enteric fever, and nursing is the only thing that cures that. He’s in China now﻿—quite an important person.”

“I should like to have that other story,” said Tab. “Kipling gets my goat. That ‘other story’ of his is never told. I think he must have had them in his mind when he referred to them, but he got lazy on it.”

“My other story must keep,” she smiled. “Some day﻿—perhaps﻿—but not now. The father of the boy laid out my little Chinese garden, by-the-way.”

===Chinese===
“哦，他后来好了——他父亲拿些稀奇古怪的成药给他灌。我想他害的是肠热病，这种病唯有精心看护才治得好。他如今在中国——是位相当有头脸的人物了。”

“那个‘另一个故事’我倒想听听。”塔布说，“吉卜林真让我来气。他那些‘另一个故事’从来就没讲出来过。我看他提起来的时候，脑子里准是装着的，只是后来懒得写了。”

“我的另一个故事且得等等。”她笑了，“哪一天——也许——可不是现在。对了，那孩子的父亲，就是布置我这座小小中国园林的人。”

===Original===
Tab had come by train and there was a long walk to the station. He stayed to the very last moment, and then had to hurry to catch the one fast express of the afternoon. He had gone a leisurely hundred yards from the front gate (you cannot walk fast if you turn around at intervals for a glimpse of a cool white figure) when he saw a dusty roadfarer coming toward him. The awkward gait of the walker, his baggy clothes, the huge Derby hat pulled down to his ears, attracted Tab’s attention long before he could distinguish the man’s features. When he did, it was with a spasm of surprise. The walker was a Chinaman and carried in his hand a flat packet.

The oriental deviated from the straight path to cross the road. Without a word he carefully unwrapped a thin paper cover and exposed a letter. It was addressed to “Miss Ursula Ardfern, Stone Cottage,” and on the wrapper Tab saw a number of Chinese characters which he guessed were directions to the messenger.

“Tell,” said the man laconically. He evidently knew little English.

===Chinese===
塔布是坐火车来的，去车站还有一段长路。他磨蹭到最后一刻，才不得不撒腿紧赶，去搭下午唯一的一班快车。出了前门，他悠悠然走出百来码（要是你不时回头，想再瞥一眼那袭清凉的白衣身影，脚步便快不起来），忽见一个满身尘土的赶路人迎面而来。那人步态别扭，衣裤臃肿，一顶大号圆顶硬礼帽直压到耳朵——还没等看清面目，这些便先攫住了塔布的注意。等看清了，他不由得心里一惊。来人是个中国人，手里拿着一只扁平的纸包。

那位东方人偏离正路，横穿过马路，一言不发，小心翼翼地揭开一层薄纸封套，露出一封信来。信封上写着“乌苏拉·阿德芬小姐，石屋”；封套上还有一行行汉字，塔布猜是写给送信人的指引。

“送信。”那人言简意赅地说。他显然不大懂英语。

===Original===
“That house on the left,” said Tab pointing. “How far have you come, Chink?”

“Velly well,” said the man, and taking the letter folded it again in its covering and trotted off.

Tab looked after him, wondering. How curious a coincidence, he thought, that they should have been talking about the Chinese only half-an-hour before?

===Chinese===
“左手那座宅子。”塔布指着说，“你打多远来的，中国佬？”

“好的。”那人应了一声，把信重新裹进封套，迈开小碎步跑去了。

塔布望着他的背影，心里纳闷。真是奇巧的巧合，他想——不过半个钟头之前，他们不正在谈论中国人吗？

===Original===
He had to run and then only caught the train as it was pulling out of the station.

The inexorable and constitutionally discontented news editor was not at all satisfied with the story as Tab wrote it.

“It loses half its value if we can’t give her name,” he complained, “and after we’ve started the interest going, the *Herald*, or some other paper, will find out who is the owner of the jewels and get all the fat of the story! Can’t you persuade her?”

Tab shook his head.

===Chinese===
他只好一路飞跑，待赶到时列车正缓缓驶出车站，好歹算是赶上了。

那位铁面无私、生来牢骚满腹的新闻主编，对塔布写成的这篇报道很不满意。

“报不出她的名字，稿子就折了一半的价。”他抱怨道，“等咱们把读者的胃口吊起来，《先驱报》（Herald）或是别的什么报纸就会查出珠宝的主人，把这条新闻的肥水全捞了去！你就不能劝劝她？”

塔布摇了摇头。

===Original===
“What’s the great idea﻿—is she going into a convent or something?”

“She didn’t mention it,” said Tab impatiently. “There it is﻿—take it or leave it, Jacques. It is a good item, and if you don’t like it, I’ll take it to the chief.”

A threat which invariably ended all discussion, for Tab was an important personage on the staff of the *Megaphone* and his word carried weight.

===Chinese===
“她这葫芦里卖的什么药——是要进修道院还是怎么的？”

“她没提。”塔布不耐烦地说，“稿子就在这儿——要不要随你，雅克（Jacques）。这是条好新闻，你要是不稀罕，我就拿到总编那儿去。”

这一威胁万试万灵，一切争论无不就此收场——因为塔布是《扩音器报》编辑部里一位举足轻重的人物，他的话自有分量。

## XIII / 十三

===Original===
Mr. Stott combined the implacable qualities of the feudal lord with an amiable leaning toward the society and approval of his fellow men. There was a café near his office which was extensively patronized by grave business men, directors, bank managers and superior cashiers. The price of luncheon had been scientifically fixed by the proprietor, so that whilst it was within the means of men of substance and standing, it was just beyond the reach of those whose limited incomes did not permit the luxury of lunching at Toby’s, though it was well worth the money to sit at meat with men who had offices labelled “private” and drove to their business in polished limousines.

Mr. Stott referred to the wistful folk who passed the door of Toby’s to be swallowed up in less exclusive establishments, as the hoi polloi, which he understood was an Italian expression. Toby’s had almost acquired the status of a club. Occasionally, ignorant strangers wandered in to test the gastronomical excellence of the kitchen, and these were usually accommodated in obscure corners away from the hearing of intimate gossip.

Mr. Stott had recently become a person to be listened to with respect, and the necessity for keeping the regular patrons of Toby’s aloof from the vulgar herd, was doubly urgent by reason of the very important matters that had to be discussed.

===Chinese===
斯托特先生身上，兼有封建领主那股不容分说的威严，又带着一副乐于同世人往还、爱听人称许的和气心肠。他事务所附近有一家餐馆，来的常客尽是些面色凝重的实业家、公司董事、银行经理和高级出纳。午餐的价钱由店主经过一番精明核算定下，恰好吃得起的是有身家有地位的人物，而进项有限、享受不起在托比家用午餐这份奢侈的人，则刚好差着一截——虽说能同那些事务所门上标着“私人”字样、乘锃亮豪华轿车上下班的人物同席共餐，这钱花得着实不冤。

那些一脸怅惘的男女在托比家门前走过，转身便被档次低些的馆子吞没，斯托特先生管这类人叫“乌合之众”（hoi polloi）——照他的理解，这是句意大利话。托比家差不多已经取得了俱乐部的地位。偶尔有无知的外乡客信步闯进来，要领教领教这里厨房烹调的高妙，这类人通常被打发到僻静的角落里落座，免得他们听见那些体己的闲话。

斯托特先生近来成了个一开口便令人肃然起敬的人物；何况还有非同小可的大事要谈，把托比家的常客同庸众隔开，便成了加倍紧迫的事。

===Original===
“What I can’t understand, Stott,” said one of his hearers, “is why the devil didn’t you send for the police?”

Mr. Stott smiled mysteriously.

“The police should have been there,” he said, “and by-the-way, I need not remind you fellows that what I say to you is in absolute confidence. I am scared out of my life lest that babbling servant of mine starts talking. You can never trust these gossiping girls. I confess, though, that I had half a mind, not to send for the police, but to tackle the Chinks myself. I should have done it, too, but the girl was so frightened of being left alone.”

===Chinese===
“有一件事我不明白，斯托特，”听众中一位说，“你究竟见鬼为什么不叫警察？”

斯托特先生神秘地微微一笑。

“警察本该到场的。”他说，“对了，用不着我提醒诸位，我对你们讲的这些话务求绝对保密。我家那个碎嘴的女仆要是开口乱讲，我能给吓掉半条命。这种好嚼舌头的丫头，你永远信不过。不过我招认，当时我倒有一半心思，不是去叫警察，而是亲自去对付那帮中国佬。我也真会那么干的，只是那姑娘怕极了一个人留下。”

===Original===
“Have they come since?” asked another interested hearer.

“No; nor the woman﻿—you remember that I told you of the woman who used to drive up to Mayfield every night in her car?”

“It seems to me that the police ought to know,” interrupted the first speaker. “One of your servants is bound to talk. As you say, you can’t trust ’em! And then the authorities will want to know why you haven’t reported the matter.”

“It is not my business,” said Mr. Stott pharisaically, “It is for the police to get busy. I’m not at all surprised that the coroner’s jury made the remark they did. Here is a man murdered﻿—”

He exhibited the crime graphically.

“At any rate, I’m keeping out of it﻿—these Chinese criminals are dangerous fellows to monkey with.”

===Chinese===
“打那以后他们又来过吗？”另一位好事的听众问。

“没有；那女人也没有来——记得吧，我同你们说起过那个女人，从前每天夜里都开着她的车到梅菲尔德去？”

“依我看，这事该让警察知道。”头一个说话的人插嘴道，“你家仆人里准有哪个会说出去的。正像你自己说的，这种人信不过！到时候官府还要追问，你为什么一直没有报案。”

“这不干我的事。”斯托特先生一副假仁假义的腔调，“该卖力气的是警察。验尸陪审团作了他们那样的评语，我一点儿也不奇怪。好好一个人被谋杀了——”

他把这桩凶案讲得绘声绘色。

“不管怎么说，我要置身事外——这些中国的罪犯，可不是好招惹的角色。”

===Original===
He had paid his bill and was walking out of the café, when somebody touched him on the arm, and he swung round to see a tall, melancholy and long faced man.

“Excuse me; Mr. Stott, I believe?”

“That is my name. I haven’t the pleasure﻿—”

“My name is Carver﻿—I am an Inspector of Police, and I want you to tell me something about what was seen outside of Mayfield, both before and after the murder.”

===Chinese===
他付过账，正走出餐馆，有人碰了碰他的胳膊。他霍地转过身，只见一个高个子、神情忧郁的长脸男人站在面前。

“冒昧打扰。您是斯托特先生吧？”

“正是贱名。恕在下眼拙，还没请教尊姓——”

“我姓卡弗——是警察探长。我要您给我讲讲，凶案发生之前和之后，梅菲尔德外面都看到了些什么动静。”

===Original===
Mr. Stott’s face fell.

“That servant of mine has been talking,” he said annoyed. “I knew she couldn’t keep her mouth shut.”

“I know nothing about your servant, sir,” said Carver, sadly, “but I have been sitting in Toby’s for the past three days and I have heard quite a lot. It sounded to me almost as if you were the principal speaker on the subject, but maybe I was mistaken.”

“I shall say nothing,” said Mr. Stott firmly, and the detective sighed.

===Chinese===
斯托特先生的脸登时垮了下来。

“准是我家那个女仆嚼了舌头。”他恼火地说，“我早知道她封不住那张嘴。”

“您家女仆的事，先生，我一概不知。”卡弗一脸愁苦地说，“只是过去三天，我一直在托比家坐着，听来的东西着实不少。在我听来，这个题目的主讲人，几乎回回都是您——不过，或许是我听岔了也未可知。”

“我一个字也不会讲的。”斯托特先生斩钉截铁地说。侦探叹了口气。

===Original===
“I shouldn’t hurry to make up my mind on that subject if I were you,” he said, “it is certain to be a difficult business explaining to the Public Prosecutor why you have kept silence so long﻿—it looks very suspicious, you know, Mr. Stott.”

Mr. Stott was aghast.

“Suspicious﻿—me﻿—Good heavens! Come to my office, Mr. Carver﻿—suspicious! I knew I should be dragged into it! I’ll fire Eline tonight!”

===Chinese===
“我要是您，就不会急着在这个问题上把主意拿死。”他说，“为什么缄默这么久，要向检察官交代清楚，准是桩难办的事——您知道，这看上去非常可疑，斯托特先生。”

斯托特先生大惊失色。

“可疑——我——老天爷！请到我事务所来谈，卡弗先生——居然说可疑！我早知道自己免不了被牵扯进去！今晚我就把艾琳开革！”

===Original===
When Tab in the course of duty, called that night at the station, he heard the story from Carver.

“If the poor nut had only had the pluck to telephone to the police when the girl first told him the story, we could have caught those birds. As it is, there’s no sense in keeping the house under observation any longer. Who was the woman? That puzzles me. Who was the woman who, night after night, garaged her car in Trasmere’s garden and let herself into the house carrying a square black bag?”

Tab did not answer. The identity of the woman was no mystery to him. She was Ursula Ardfern.

===Chinese===
塔布当晚因公到警察局去，从卡弗口中听说了这段情由。

“那可怜的糊涂虫，当初姑娘头一回讲起这事时，但凡有胆量给警察打个电话，那两个家伙我们早就逮着了。如今事已至此，再派人盯那所宅子的梢已毫无意义。那女人是谁？这可让我犯疑。一夜又一夜把车存进特拉斯米尔家花园、提着一只方形黑提包自己开门进屋的，究竟是个什么女人？”

塔布没有答话。那女人的身份，在他并不是什么谜。她是乌苏拉·阿德芬。

===Original===
The fabric of supposition fitted piece to piece. He remembered how he had come upon her in the deserted streets at dawn surveying a burst tyre and the plainness of her dress. Inside the car was a square black case, but﻿—

Ursula working hand in glove with Chinamen; Ursula privy to these stealthy coming and goings, these midnight burglaries at Mayfield? That was unthinkable.

“﻿—their reason for breaking in after we had left the place is beyond me,” Carver was saying. “I can only suppose that they hoped that we had overlooked something of value.”

===Chinese===
推测的图样一榫一卯都对上了。他记得，自己如何在破晓时分空无一人的街头撞见她——她正打量着一只爆了的轮胎，一身衣着素净无华。车里放着的，恰是一只方形黑匣——可是——

乌苏拉同中国人狼狈为奸；乌苏拉是这些鬼祟出入的同谋，是梅菲尔德这一场场午夜盗窃的知情人？这不可想象。

“——我们撤走之后他们为什么还要破门而入，我想不明白。”卡弗这时正说着，“我只能猜想，他们是指望我们漏看了什么值钱的东西。”

===Original===
“In Mayfield?﻿—there is nothing there now?”

“Only the furniture and one or two articles we took away but have since returned, such as the green lacquer box. As a matter of fact, they only went back yesterday. Mr. Lander thought of selling all the furniture and effects by auction, and I believe that before he left he put the matter in the hands of an agent. The Chinamen intrigue me,” he said, “though it is by no means certain that both Stott and his servant aren’t mistaken. I gather they were considerably panic stricken and even I wouldn’t undertake to distinguish a Chinaman from a European by the light of a match.”

Tab went up into Carver’s private office, and they sat talking until close on eleven o’clock, at which hour their conversation was violently interrupted by the ring of the telephone.

===Chinese===
“在梅菲尔德？——那里如今还有什么东西？”

“只剩家具，还有一两件我们带走、后来又送回去的东西，比如那只绿漆盒子。其实，东西昨天才送回去。兰德先生有意把全部家具什物拍卖掉，我相信他动身之前，已把这件事托给了一位经纪人。那两个中国人很让我纳闷，”他说，“不过，斯托特和他家女仆是不是都看走了眼，也远不能说定。我料想他们当时吓得不轻，就算换了我，凭一根火柴的光，也不敢担保分得清哪个是中国人、哪个是欧洲人。”

塔布上楼进了卡弗的私人办公室，两人坐着一直谈到将近十一点。就在这个钟点，一阵急促的电话铃声把他们的谈话猛然打断了。

===Original===
“Call through for you, sir,” said the voice of the sergeant on the desk, and a second later Carver recognised the agitated voice of Mr. Stott.

“They’re here now! They’ve just gone in! The woman has opened the door﻿—they’ve just gone in!”

“Who? Is that Stott﻿—do you mean into Mayfield?” asked Carver quickly.

“Yes! I saw them with my own eyes. The woman’s car is outside the door.”

===Chinese===
“先生，您的电话接进来了。”值台警士的声音说。片刻之后，卡弗听出了斯托特先生气急败坏的嗓音。

“他们这会儿来了！刚刚进去！那女人开了门——他们刚刚进去！”

“谁？是斯托特吗？——你是说，进梅菲尔德去了？”卡弗急问。

“对！我亲眼看见的。那女人的车就停在门口。”

===Original===
“Go and get its number, quick,” said Carver sharply, “find a policeman and tell him, and if you can’t find one, detain the woman yourself.”

He heard Mr. Stott’s feeble expostulation, and jumped for his hat.

They boarded the first taxicab they could find, and raced through the town at a breakneck pace, turning into one end of the quiet avenue in which Mayfield was situated, just as the tail lights of a car turned the corner at the other end.

===Chinese===
“去，把车牌号抄下来，快！”卡弗厉声道，“找个警察告诉他；找不着的话，你就自己把那女人扣住。”

他听着斯托特先生有气无力的推托，纵身去抓帽子。

两人跳上能找到的头一辆出租车，不要命地穿城飞驰，拐进梅菲尔德所在那条僻静大街的这一头时，正赶上望见一辆汽车的尾灯从那一头的街角拐了过去。

===Original===
Mr. Stott was standing on the sidewalk, pointing dumbly, but with hysterical gestures, at the place where the car had been.

“They’ve gone,” he said hollowly. “﻿—couldn’t find a policeman: they’ve gone!”

“So I notice,” said Carver. “Did you take the number of the car?”

Mr. Stott shook his head and made a choking noise in his throat. Presently he commanded his speech.

“Covered over with black paper,” he said.

===Chinese===
斯托特先生站在人行道上，朝着汽车原先停的地方，一声不吭、歇斯底里地指指戳戳。

“他们走了。”他嗓音发空地说，“——找不着警察：他们走了！”

“可不是。”卡弗说，“车牌号记下了吗？”

斯托特先生摇了摇头，喉咙里发出一声哽咽。好一会儿，他才把话音拿稳。

“拿黑纸糊住了。”他说。

===Original===
“Who was it?”

“A Chinaman and a woman,” said the other.

“Why in hell didn’t you stop them?” snapped Carver.

“A Chinaman and a woman,” repeated Stott miserably.

“What was she like?”

“I didn’t get near enough to see,” Mr. Stott made the confession without shame. “There ought to have been police here﻿—lots of police﻿—. It is disgraceful. I am going to write to the﻿—”

===Chinese===
“来的是什么人？”

“一个中国人，还有个女人。”另一位说。

“见鬼，你为什么不把他们拦住？”卡弗厉声喝道。

“一个中国人，还有个女人。”斯托特可怜巴巴地又念了一遍。

“她什么模样？”

“我没凑到近前，没看清。”斯托特先生坦白得毫无愧色。“这儿本该有警察——大批的警察——。简直不成体统。我要写信给——”

===Original===
They left him quivering threats. Carver ran across the concrete garden, unlocked the door and switched on all the lights in the hall. Nothing, so far as he could see, had been disturbed. The door to the vault was locked, and had not been tampered with. Apparently the dining-room had. The fireplace of the house was a broad deep cavity lined with red brick, and pointed with a yellow cement. An electric radiator had replaced the stove, and Carter had made a very thorough examination both of the recess and of the wide chimney above. But he saw at a glance that his inspection had been short of perfect. One of the bricks had been taken out. It lay on the table, with its steel lid open, and Carver surveyed it thoughtfully.

“That is one on me,” he said. “It looks like the face of a brick, doesn’t it? Look at that artistic cement pointing all round the edge? It isn’t cement at all, but steel. In fact, this must be about the only secret drawer in the house. I ought to have made more thorough enquiries from the builders.”

The box was empty except for a tiny rubber band. They found its fellow on the table.

===Chinese===
两人把他撂在那里，任他抖抖索索地放狠话。卡弗快步穿过水泥铺面的前园，开锁进门，把门厅里的灯统统拧亮。依他看，样样东西都原封未动。通保险库的门锁着，没人动过手脚；餐厅的门显然被动过了。宅子的壁炉是个宽阔深陷的凹腔，内里衬砌红砖，砖缝用黄水泥勾缝。电暖炉早已取代了原来的炉灶，这处凹龛连同上方宽大的烟道，卡弗当初都做过十分彻底的检查。可他一眼便看出，自己那番查验还欠着火候。有一块砖被起了出来，躺在桌上，钢制的小盖敞开着。卡弗对着它，若有所思地端详。

“这一手算我栽了。”他说，“瞧着活脱是一面砖，是不是？看看四缘那一圈勾缝勾得多讲究？根本不是水泥，是钢。说穿了，这宅子里约莫就只这一个暗屉。当初真该向营造商问得更透彻些。”

匣子里空空如也，只剩一根小小的橡皮筋；另一根，他们在桌上找到了。

===Original===
“There was something of importance in that box which has been taken out; probably a bundle of papers, more likely two bundles. The rubber bands suggest two. Anyway, they’re gone.”

He glanced around the room.

“And the green lacquer box has gone,” he said. “I know it was here, because I put it on the mantelshelf with my own hands.”

He opened the door leading to the vault and satisfied himself that nobody had gained admission to the underground room.

===Chinese===
“匣子里原先装着要紧东西，被人取走了；多半是一捆文件，更可能是两捆。橡皮筋有两根，正说明是两捆。不管怎样，东西没了。”

他朝房间里环视了一圈。

“还有，那只绿漆盒子也不见了。”他说，“我知道它先前就在这儿，因为是我亲手把它搁上壁炉台的。”

他打开通往保险库的那扇门，确认没有任何人进过那间地下屋子。

===Original===
“We had better go along and see this police critic,” he said grimly.

It appeared that he had done Mr. Stott an injustice, for greatly fearing, he had crossed the road whilst the people were in the house, and he had made honest attempts to find a policeman, having sent the toothachy Eline on that errand, which was successful, if the success was somewhat belated, for the policeman arrived with her whilst the Inspector was talking to the merchant.

“I not only crossed the road,” said Mr. Stott, “but I went inside the garden. They must have seen me, for the light in the dining-room went out suddenly, and they came flying down the steps together.”

“And passed you, of course?”

“They did not pass me,” explained Mr. Stott emphatically, “because I was on the other side of the road before they were out of the gate. I do not think anything would have passed me.”

===Chinese===
“咱们还是过去会一会这位警察批评家吧。”他冷冷地说。

看来，先前倒是错怪了斯托特先生。他虽然怕得厉害，趁那些人还在宅子里时，当真穿过马路去过，还实实在在设法找过警察——派了牙疼的艾琳去跑这趟差，而且差事办成了，只是成功得晚了些：探长正同这位商人说话时，那警察才随她一同赶到。

“我不光穿过了马路，”斯托特先生说，“我还进到花园里去了。他们准是瞧见了我，因为餐厅的灯忽然灭了，两人便一齐飞奔着下了台阶。”

“那自然是从您身边跑过去的了？”

“他们没从我身边过。”斯托特先生加重语气解释道，“因为他们还没出大门，我人已经站到马路对面去了。依我看，什么东西也休想从我身边过去。”

===Original===
“What was the woman like?” asked Carver again.

“I have an idea she was young, but I did not see her face. She was dressed in black and as far as I could see, veiled. The other man was small; he only came up to her shoulder.”

“That is that,” said Carver disconsolately, when they came away. “They ought to have been caught, if that man had the spunk of a rabbit. You are very silent, Tab﻿—what are you thinking?”

“I am wondering,” said Tab truthfully, “just wondering.”

“What are you wondering?” growled the other.

“I am wondering whether old Trasmere was a much worse man than any of us imagine,” said Tab calmly.

===Chinese===
“那女人什么模样？”卡弗又问了一遍。

“我有个印象，她年轻，可我没看清她的脸。她一身黑衣，就我所能辨出的，脸上蒙着面纱。另外那个男人个子矮小，只齐到她的肩头。”

“就这么回事了。”两人离开时，卡弗怏怏地说。“他们本该被逮住的，只要那家伙有兔子那么一丁点胆量。你怎么一声不吭，塔布——在想什么呢？”

“我在琢磨事儿，”塔布说的是实话，“就是琢磨而已。”

“琢磨什么呢？”对方瓮声瓮气地问。

“我在想，”塔布平静地说，“老特拉斯米尔这个人，是不是比我们谁都料想的还要坏得多。”

## XIV / 十四

===Original===

Early in the morning Tab paid a fruitless visit to Stone Cottage. The woman who acted as caretaker told him that the young lady had returned to town, and it was at the Central Hotel that he saw her.

Never had he approached an enquiry, professional or otherwise, with such reluctance. On most matters Tab had very definite views. His mentality was such that he never hesitated to form a judgment, or wavered in his convictions. That type of mind cannot understand in others the vacillating hesitancy, which so often distinguishes them in their judgment of people and things. And yet, strive as he did, he could not reduce to a formula, his own chaotic feelings in relation to Ursula Ardfern. One thing he knew. It was no vicarious interest he was showing﻿—he did not even in his own mind regard himself as standing for Rex Lander.

Tab thought best with a pen in his hand, yet when in cold blood he endeavoured to reduce to writing the exact state of his mind in relation to Ursula Ardfern, the white sheet of paper remained white to the end.

The moment he entered her sitting-room, Tab felt that Ursula knew the object of his visit.

===Chinese===

一大早，塔布去石屋跑了一趟，徒劳无功。看房的女人告诉他，小姐已经回城里去了；他见到她，正是在中央饭店。

无论职业的还是别的什么调查，他从来没有这样勉强地着手过。在大多数事情上，塔布的看法都极有定见。他那副头脑，向来是判断下得干脆、信念摇不动分毫。正是这路头脑，无法理解旁人身上那种摇摆不定的犹疑——而旁人判断起人与事来，偏偏常常带着这种犹疑。然而，任凭他怎样绞脑汁，也把自己对乌苏拉·阿德芬那团乱麻似的情感归纳不成一条公式。有一件事他是明白的：他此刻上心，绝非代人尽力的兴味——他连在心里都不曾把自己看作是替雷克斯·兰德出头的。

塔布手里握着笔时思路最清，可等他冷静下来，想把自个儿对乌苏拉·阿德芬的心境原原本本落到纸上时，那张白纸到头来还是白纸一张。

一踏进她的客厅，塔布便觉出乌苏拉已经知道他此来的目的。

===Original===

“You want to see me very badly, don’t you?” she said, without preliminary, and he nodded.

“What is it?”

Unless he was dreaming, her voice held a subtle caress, and yet that was a ridiculous exaggeration: perhaps “kindness” were a better word.

“Somebody went into Mayfield last night, accompanied by a Chinaman, and they got away just before the police arrived,” said Tab awkwardly, “and that isn’t all; that same somebody has been in the habit of visiting Trasmere between eleven at night and two in the morning, and this practice has been going on for a considerable time.”

She nodded.

“I told you I did not know Mr. Trasmere,” she said quietly. “It is the only lie I have told you. I knew Mr. Trasmere very well, but there were reasons why it would have been fatal for me to have admitted my friendship with him. No, not one lie﻿—two.” She held up her fingers to emphasize her words.

===Chinese===

“你有话急着要见我，是不是？”她连开场白都没有。他点了点头。

“什么事？”

若不是他自己神志恍惚，她的嗓音里便含着一缕微妙的温存——可这么说又是可笑的夸张：或许“和善”才是个更妥帖的字眼。

“昨天夜里，有人进过梅菲尔德，身边还带着个中国人，赶在警察到之前一刻才脱身，”塔布说得讷讷的，“这还不算；同一个人的老习惯，是在夜里十一点到凌晨两点之间拜访特拉斯米尔，而且这种做法已经持续了相当的时候。”

她点了点头。

“我告诉过你，我不认识特拉斯米尔先生，”她平静地说，“那是我对你撒过的唯一的谎。其实我同特拉斯米尔先生熟得很，只是有种种缘由，若承认了同他的交情，对我便是致命的。不，不是一句谎——是两句。”她竖起手指，加重自己的话。

===Original===

“The other was about the lost jewel-case,” said Tab huskily.

“Yes,” she replied.

“You didn’t lose it at all.”

She shook her head.

“No, I didn’t lose it at all; I knew where it was all the time, but I was﻿—panic stricken, and had to make a decision on the spur of the moment. I do not regret it.”

There was a pause.

===Chinese===

“另一句，是关于那只丢失的首饰盒。”塔布声音发哑地说。

“是的。”她答道。

“你压根儿就没丢。”

她摇了摇头。

“是的，我压根儿没丢；它在哪儿，我自始至终都清楚。只是我当时——吓得慌了神，只得仓促之间拿个主意。我并不后悔。”

沉默了片刻。

===Original===

“Do the police know?” she asked.

“About you? No. I think they might find out﻿—not from me.”

“Sit down.” She was very calm. He thought she was going to explain, and was quite satisfied that the explanation was a very simple one, but she had no such intention, as her first words told him.

“I can’t tell you now the why of everything. I am too﻿—what is the word? Too tense. I am not so sure that that is the word, either, but my defences are in being. I dare not relax one of them, or the whole would go. Of course, I knew nothing of the murder﻿—you never dreamt I did?”

He shook his head.

“I did not know until Sunday morning, when I was driving out to Stone Cottage,” she said. “It was only by accident that I bought a paper in the street, and then I made my decision. I went straight to the police station with my story of the lost jewel-case. I knew it was in the vault and I had to find some explanation.”

===Chinese===

“警察知道吗？”她问。

“关于你？不知道。我想他们兴许会查出来——但不会是从我这里。”

“坐下。”她十分镇定。他以为她要解释了，而且满心以为那解释必定十分简单，可她压根儿没这个打算，头一句话便让他明白了。

“眼下我还不能把一切的缘由告诉你。我太——那个词怎么说来着？太紧绷了。我也不敢说就是这个词，反正我的防线全都架着，一道也不敢松，松了一道就全线崩溃。当然，那桩凶案我一无所知——你总不至于梦想到是我干的吧？”

他摇了摇头。

“直到礼拜天早上，我驱车去石屋的路上才知道，”她说，“我在街上买报纯属偶然，看过便拿定了主意。我揣着首饰盒遗失的那套说辞，径直去了警察局。我知道它就在保险库里，总得找个说法。”

===Original===

“How did it come to be in the vault?” Tab knew that the question was futile before it was half out.

“That is part of the other story,” she smiled faintly. “Do you believe me?”

He looked up at her quickly, and their eyes met.

“Does it matter whether I believe or not?” he asked quietly.

“It matters a great deal to me,” she said in the same tone.

It was his gaze that fell first.

===Chinese===

“它怎么会进保险库的呢？”这话还没说出一半，塔布就知道是白问。

“那是另一桩事里的一环。”她淡淡一笑。“你信我吗？”

他霍然抬眼看她，两人的目光相遇了。

“我信不信，要紧吗？”他轻声问。

“对我来说要紧得很。”她用同样的声调说。

先垂下目光的是他。

===Original===

Then in a different and more cheery voice, she went on:

“You have to help me, Mr. Tab. Not in the matter we have been discussing﻿—I don’t mean that.”

“I’ll help you in that,” said Tab.

“I think you will,” she answered quickly, “but for the moment, ungracious as it may sound, I do not need help. The other matter is more personal. Do you remember telling me about your friend?”

“Rex?” he asked in surprise.

She nodded.

===Chinese===

随后她换了副明快些的嗓音，接着说：

“你得帮帮我，塔布先生。不是咱们方才谈的那桩事——我不是那个意思。”

“那桩事我也帮。”塔布说。

“我想你会的，”她应得很快，“只是眼下，说来显得不知好歹，我还用不着人帮。另一桩事更要紧些，是私事。你可记得跟我讲起过你那位朋友？”

“雷克斯？”他愕然问。

她点了点头。

===Original===

“He went to Naples, didn’t he? I had a letter from him written on board.”

Tab smiled.

“Poor old Rex. What did he want, your photograph?”

“More than that,” she said quietly. “You won’t think I am horrible if I betray his confidence, but I must, if you are to help me. Mr. Lander has done me the honour of asking me to marry him.”

===Chinese===

“他去那不勒斯（Naples）了，是吧？我收到过他船上写来的一封信。”

塔布笑了。

“可怜的老雷克斯。他想要什么，要你的照片？”

“不止这个，”她平静地说，“我要是辜负他这份信任，你可别觉得我面目可憎，可你必须帮我，我就只得说了。兰德先生垂青，向我求婚了。”

===Original===

Tab looked at her open-mouthed.

“Rex?” he said incredulously.

She nodded.

“I won’t show you the letter, it would hardly be fair; but he has asked me to give my answer in the agony column of the *Megaphone*. He says that he has an agent in London who will send it by wireless, and I was wondering﻿—” she hesitated.

“If I were the agent?” said Tab, “No, I know nothing whatever about this.”

She drew a sigh.

===Chinese===

塔布张着嘴望她。

“雷克斯？”他不肯相信似的说。

她点了点头。

“信我就不给你看了，那对他未免不公平；他要我把答复登在《扩音器报》的启事专栏里。他说他在伦敦有个代理人，会用无线电报给他发过去。我方才还在琢磨——”她迟疑了一下。

“琢磨我就是那个代理人？”塔布说，“不，这事我一无所知。”

她舒了一口气。

===Original===

“I’m glad,” she said inconsequently. “I mean, I’m glad that you won’t be hurt even indirectly.”

“Do you intend putting in the advertisement?”

“I have already sent it to the paper,” she said. “Here is a copy.” She went to her writing-table and brought back a slip of paper and Tab read:

> **“Rex:** What you ask is quite impossible. I shall never make any other reply.
> U.”

“One does get that kind of letters,” she said, “and as a rule they are not worth while answering. Had I not known he was a friend of yours, I don’t think I should have taken the trouble﻿—yes I would,” she nodded slowly. “Mr. Trasmere’s nephew has certain claims to refusal.”

“Poor old Rex,” said Tab softly. “I had a wireless from him this morning saying that he was enjoying the voyage.”

===Chinese===

“我很高兴，”她没头没尾地说，“我是说，你连间接受点儿伤都不会，我很高兴。”

“你打算登那条启事？”

“我已经寄给报社了，”她说，“这儿有一份底稿。”她走到写字台前，取回一张纸条，塔布念道：

> **“雷克斯：你所求的事绝无可能。我不会有任何别的答复。
> 乌。”**

“谁都会收到那样的信，”她说，“照例是犯不着回的。若不是知道他是你的朋友，我想我不会费这个事——不，我也会费，”她缓缓点头，“特拉斯米尔先生的侄子嘛，求到了头上，总该赏他一个回绝。”

“可怜的老雷克斯，”塔布轻声说，“今早我还收到他的无线电报，说这一路航行很惬意。”

===Original===

He took up his hat.

“As regards the other matter, Miss Ardfern,” he said, “you must tell me in your own time, if you wish to tell me at all. But you must understand that there is a very big chance that the police will trace you, in which case I may be of assistance. As matters stand, I am just a sympathetic observer.”

He held out his hand with a smile and she took it and held it in both of hers.

“For twelve years I have been living in a nightmare,” she said, “a nightmare which my own vanity created. I think I am awake now, and when the police trace me﻿—and I am so certain they will trace me that I have left the stage﻿—”

“Was that the reason?” he exclaimed in surprise.

“That is one of the two reasons,” she said. “When they trace me, I think I shall be glad. There is still something of the old Eve in me,” she smiled a little sadly, “to make exposure a painful possibility.”

===Chinese===

他拿起帽子。

“至于另一桩事，阿德芬小姐，”他说，“你想说的时候再说就是，倘若终究愿意说的话。可你必须明白，警察很有可能查到你，真到那时，我兴许还帮得上忙。就目前的情形而言，我不过是个抱有同情的旁观者。”

他含笑伸出手去，她握住，用双手捧着。

“十二年来，我一直活在一场噩梦里，”她说，“一场由我自己的虚荣造出来的噩梦。我想我如今醒了。等警察查到我——我笃定他们查得到，笃定到我已经退了舞台——”

“就是为了这个才退的？”他失声问。

“那是两个缘由之一，”她说，“等他们查到我，我想我会高兴的。我身上到底还留着几分老夏娃的影子，”她笑得有几分凄然，“叫这曝光成了一种痛苦的可能。”

===Original===

One last question he asked as he stood at the door.

“What was in the box? The box that looked like a brick and was hidden in the fireplace?”

“Papers,” she replied. “I only know they were papers written in Chinese. I do not know what they were about yet.”

“Had they﻿—could they possibly supply a clue to the murderer?”

She shook her head and he was satisfied.

He smiled at her and with no other word, went out. All doubts that he had had as to his feeling toward her were now set at rest. He loved this slim girl with the madonna-like face, whose moods changed as swiftly as April light. He did not think of Rex, or the heartache which her message would bring, until later.

===Chinese===

站在门口，他问了最后一个问题。

“那盒子里是什么？那只看上去像块砖、藏在壁炉里的盒子？”

“是纸件，”她答道，“我只知道是些用中文写就的纸。写的是什么，我至今不知道。”

“它们——可不可能提供凶手的线索？”

她摇了摇头，他也就放了心。

他朝她一笑，再没多说一句话，走了出去。此前他对自己的心意曾有过的种种疑惑，此刻统统安顿了。他爱这个圣母般面孔的苗条姑娘，她的心绪像四月的天光，说变就变。至于雷克斯，至于她那条启事将带来的心痛，他都是后来才想起的。

===Original===

There was no very satisfactory portrait of Wellington Brown in existence. On the ship which brought him from China, a fellow passenger had taken a snapshot of a group in which Mr. Brown’s face, slightly out of focus, loomed foggily. With this to work on, and with the assistance of Tab, something like a near-portrait was constructed and circulated by the police. Every newspaper carried the portrait, every amateur detective in the country was looking for the man with the beard, whose gloves had been found outside the death chamber of Jesse Trasmere.

Less fortunate was the lot of Mr. Walter Felling, alias Walters. He had been in prison, and his portraits, full face and profile, were available for immediate distribution. He watched the hunt from one of those densely crowded burrows where humanity swelters and festers on the hot days and nights. In the top room of a crowded tenement, he grew more and more gaunt as the days went by, for the fear of death was in his heart.

Despite the efficient portraiture it is doubtful whether he would have been recognized by the most lynx-eyed policeman, for his beard had reached a considerable length and suspense and terror had wasted his plump cheeks into hollows and cavities that had changed the very contours of his face. He knew the law; its fatal readiness to accept the most fragmentary evidence when a man was on trial for murder. His very movement had been an acknowledgment of guilt, would be accepted as such by a judge who would lay out the damning points, against him with a cold and remorseless thoroughness.

Sometimes at night, especially on rainy nights, he would creep out into the streets. Always they seemed to be full of police﻿—he would return in a panic to spend another restless night, when every creak of the stairs, every muffled voice in the rooms below made him jump to the door.

===Chinese===

现存的威灵顿·布朗照片，没有一张差强人意。在他从中国搭来的那班船上，一位同船乘客拍过一张合影快照，布朗先生的脸在里头略微失焦，雾蒙蒙地浮着。警方就凭这张底子，又有塔布从旁协助，拼出一幅近似真容的画像，翻印散发。每家报纸都登了这幅画像，全国的每一名业余侦探都在找这个留胡子的人——他的手套，正是在杰西·特拉斯米尔那间死亡之室的外面发现的。

沃尔特·费林先生——化名沃尔特斯——的运气可就差了。他蹲过监狱，正面侧面两张照片现成摆着，立等可发。他从一处那种拥挤不堪的巢穴里观望着这场围捕——一到炎热的日夜，人们便挤在那里面蒸熬溃烂。在一栋人满为患的公寓顶楼，他一天比一天形容枯槁，因为对死亡的恐惧揣在他心里。

画像固然灵通，可即便撞上目光赛过山猫的最尖的警察，他会不会被认出来也难说：他的胡子已经蓄得很长，惶惧把他原本丰润的两颊削出一片坑洼窟窿，连脸的轮廓都改了模样。他懂法律：一个人因谋杀受审时，法律那股致命的脾性——最支离破碎的证据也照单全收。他这一走本身，就等于认了罪，法官必然照此采信，还会把一条条定罪的要点，冷酷而不留情面地、周密地摆到他面前。

有时夜里，尤其是雨夜，他会偷偷溜到街上。街上仿佛永远布满警察——他便心惊肉跳地折回来，再熬一个无眠之夜；楼梯每一声吱呀，楼下每一句闷闷的人语，都吓得他一跃而起，扑到门边。

===Original===

Walters had doubled back to town, the only safe place of refuge. In the country he would have been a marked man and his liberty of short duration. Avoiding the districts which knew him well, and the friends whose loyalty would not stand the test of a murder charge, he came to the noisy end of Reed Street, posing as an out-of-work engineer.

Here he read every newspaper which he could procure, and in each journal every line that dealt with the murder. What had Wellington Brown to do with it? The appearance of that man in the case bewildered him. He remembered the visitor from China very well. So he, too, was a fugitive. The knowledge brought him a shade of comfort. It was as though a little of the burden of suspicion had been lifted from himself.

One night when he was taking the air, a Chinaman went pad-padding past him and he recognized Yeh Ling. The proprietor of the Golden Roof was one of the few Chinamen in town who seldom wore European dress, and Walters knew him. Yeh Ling had come to Mayfield on several occasions. He had worn European dress then and had excited no surprise, for Mr. Trasmere’s association with the Far East was well known. Yeh Ling must have seen him, for he had passed at a moment when the light of a street lamp fell upon Walters’ face. But he made no sign of recognition and the fugitive hoped that Yeh Ling had been absorbed in his thoughts. Nevertheless, he hurried home again to sit in his darkened room and start painfully at every sound.

Had he known that Yeh Ling had both seen and identified him, he would not have slept at all that night. The Chinaman pursued his course to the unsavoury end of Reed Street; children who saw him screamed derisively; a frowsy old woman standing in a doorway yelled a crude witticism, but Yeh Ling passed on unmoved. Turning sharply into a narrow alleyway, he stopped before a darkened shop and tapped upon a side door. It was opened at once and he passed into a thick and pungent darkness. A voice hissed a question and he answered in the same dialect. Then, without guide, he made his way up the shaky stairs to a back room.

===Chinese===

沃尔特斯兜了个圈子折回城里——唯一安全的藏身之地。若在乡间，他就是个显眼的目标，自由长不了。他避开熟识他的那些地段，也避开那些交情经不起谋杀罪名一试的朋友，落脚到里德街喧闹的一头，冒充一个失业的工程师。

在这里，凡弄得到手的报纸他每份都读，每份报里但凡与凶案相干的每行字都读。威灵顿·布朗跟这事有什么相干？此人现身案中，叫他摸不着头脑。那位从中国来的访客他记得很清楚。原来他也在逃。这个认识给他带来一丝安慰，仿佛疑心这副担子，从他自己肩上卸下去了一点。

一天夜里他出来透气，一个中国人踏着啪嗒啪嗒的碎步从他身边走过，他认出是叶凌。“金顶楼”的老板，是城里很少穿西式衣裳的那几个中国人之一，沃尔特斯认得他。叶凌来过梅菲尔德好几回。那时他穿的是西式衣裳，也没惹人惊奇，因为特拉斯米尔先生同远东的渊源尽人皆知。叶凌必定看见他了，因为他走过时，一盏路灯的光正照在沃尔特斯脸上。可他没露一点认出来的表示，这个逃犯只盼叶凌方才正自出神。饶是如此，他还是急匆匆赶回家，坐进自己那间黑灯瞎火的屋子，一有声响便惊跳，跳得心口发疼。

他若是知道叶凌不但看见了他、还认出了他，那一夜就根本合不上眼了。那个中国人径自朝里德街腌臜的一头走去；望见他的孩子起哄怪叫，一个立在门口的邋遢老太婆嚎了句粗俗的俏皮话，叶凌却无动于衷地走了过去。他猛地拐进一条窄巷，在一间黑了灯的铺子前站定，叩了叩一扇侧门。门应声而开，他跨进去，没入一片浓稠刺鼻的黑暗。一个声音嘶声问了一句，他用同样的方言答了。随后不用人引路，他摸上摇摇晃晃的楼梯，进了后屋。

===Original===

It was illuminated by the light of four candles. The walls were covered by a cheap paper, its crude design mellowed by age, and the only furniture in the room was a broad divan on which sat a compatriot, a wizened old Chinaman who was engaged in carving a half-shaped block of ivory which he held between his knees.

They greeted one another soberly and the old man uttered a mechanical politeness.

“Yo Len Fo,” said Yeh Ling, “is the man well?”

Yo Len Fo shook his head affirmatively.

===Chinese===

屋里点着四支蜡烛照明。墙上糊着廉价的墙纸，粗糙的花样让年月沤得发暗了；屋里唯一的家具是一张宽大的矮榻，榻上坐着一位同胞——一个干瘦的中国老头，正夹着两膝，雕一块半成形的象牙。

两人神色淡淡地互道了礼，老头客套了一句，照例是那一句。

“尤连福（Yo Len Fo），”叶凌说，“那人还好么？”

尤连福点头称是。

===Original===

“He is well, excellency,” he said. “He has been sleeping all the afternoon and he has just taken three pipes. He has also drunk the whiskey you sent.”

“I will see him,” said Yeh Ling and dropped some money upon the divan.

The old man picked this up, uncurled himself and putting down his ivory carefully, led the way up another flight of stairs. A small oil lamp burnt on the bare mantelpiece of the room into which Yeh Ling walked. On a discoloured mattress lay a man. He wore only shirt and trousers and his feet were bare. By the side of the mattress was a tray on which rested a pipe, a half-emptied glass and a watch.

Mr. Wellington Brown looked up at the visitor, his glazed eyes showing the faintest light of interest.

“ ’Lo, Yeh Ling﻿—come to smoke?”

His language was a queer mixture of Cantonese and English, and it was in the former tongue that Yeh Ling replied.

===Chinese===

“他很好，大人，”他说，“睡了一下午，刚抽过三筒烟。您送来的威士忌，他也喝了。”

“我去看看他。”叶凌说着，往榻上丢下几枚钱。

老头把钱拾起来，舒展开蜷着的身子，小心放下象牙，引路又上了一截楼梯。叶凌走进的那间屋里，光秃秃的壁炉架上点着一盏小油灯。一张污渍斑斑的床垫上躺着个男人，只穿着衬衫长裤，光着一双脚。床垫边搁着一只托盘，盘上一杆烟枪、一只半空的酒杯、一块表。

威灵顿·布朗先生抬眼望向来客，呆滞的眼珠里透出一丝极淡的兴味。

“喽，叶凌——来抽一口？”

他这话是粤语夹英语的怪混合，叶凌答话用的也是前者。

===Original===

“I do not smoke, Hsien,” he said and the man chuckled.

“Hsien?﻿—‘The Unemployed One,’ eh﻿—funny how names stick﻿—wasser time?”

“It is late,” said Yeh Ling, and the head of the man drooped.

“See ol’ Jesse tomorrow﻿—” he said drowsily, “got﻿—lot of business﻿—”

Yeh Ling stooped and his slim fingers encircled the man’s wrist. The pulse was weak but regular.

“It is good,” he said, turning to the old carver of ivory. “Every morning there must be air in this room. No other smoker must come, you understand, Yo Len Fo? He must be kept here.”

===Chinese===

“我不吸烟，闲公（Hsien）。”他说。那人咯咯笑了。

“闲公？——就是‘闲汉一条’，呃？——真怪，这名号就这么粘上了——现在、几点啦？”

“不早了。”叶凌说。那人的脑袋耷拉下去。

“明儿见老杰西——”他迷迷糊糊地说，“有——一大摊子事——”

叶凌俯下身，细长的手指搭上那人的腕子。脉息弱，但齐整。

“这样就好。”他转向那个雕象牙的老头说，“每天早上这屋必须通口风。别的烟客不许来，你明白么，尤连福？必须把他留在这儿。”

===Original===

“This morning he wanted to go out,” said the keeper of the establishment.

“He will stay for a long time. I know him. When he was on the Amur River, he did not leave his house for three months. Let there be one pipe always ready. Obey.”

He went softly down the stairs and into the night.

Only once did he glance back as he made his unhurried way to the side door of the Golden Roof. But that glance was sufficient. The man he had seen loafing at the entrance of the alleyway was watching him. He saw him now walking on the other side of the road, a dim, secretive figure. Yeh Ling slipped into his private door, bent down and raised the flap of a letter-slot. The man had come to a halt on the other side of the road. The reflected light from the blazing signs on the main street illuminated his back, but his face was in shadow.

===Chinese===

“今天早上他还想出去来着。”看管这处的人说。

“他会待上很久的。我了解他。当年在阿穆尔河上，他整整三个月没出过家门。烟要随时备好一筒。照办。”

他轻轻下了楼，走进夜色里。

他不慌不忙地朝“金顶楼”的侧门走去，途中只回头看了一眼。可这一眼就足够了。方才他见过的那个在巷口闲荡的男人，正在盯他的梢。此刻他看见那人走在马路对面，一个影影绰绰、鬼鬼祟祟的身影。叶凌侧身进了自己的便门，俯身掀开信箱口的活动盖。那人已在对街站定。主街上通明招牌的反光映亮他的背影，脸却埋在暗处。

===Original===

“It is not a policeman,” said Yeh Ling softly and then as the man strolled back into the darkness, he called his stunted servant.

“Follow that man who wears a cap. You will see him on the other side of the road, he is walking toward the houses of the noisy women.”

A quarter of an hour later the stunted man came back with a story of failure and Yeh Ling was not surprised. But the watcher was neither policeman nor reporter, of this he was sure.

===Chinese===

“不是警察。”叶凌低声说。待那人踱回黑暗里，他把矮小的仆人唤了来。

“盯住那个戴便帽的。你在马路对面能看见他，他正朝喧闹女人们的宅子那边走。”

一刻钟后，矮个子仆人回来了，带回一番白跑的回话，叶凌并不意外。但那盯梢的既不是警察，也不是记者——这一点他笃定。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===

Mr. Wellington Brown woke one morning feeling extraordinarily refreshed. Usually he woke with a clouded brain and a parched mouth, with no other desire than to satisfy that craving for opium which all his life had kept him poor and eventually had ruined him physically and morally. But on this occasion he opened his eyes, made a quick stock of his surroundings, and uttered a “faugh!” of disgust. He knew himself so well, and was so well acquainted with his idiosyncrasies and the character of these fits which came upon him, that he saw that the end of a bout had come. Some day he would not wake up feeling refreshed, or wake up at all.
He sat up in bed, fingering his beard, and sucked in the breeze that came through the open window. Rising to his feet, he found his knees a little unstable, and laughed foolishly. It was Yo Len Fo himself who came in bearing a tray with a glass of water, a bottle half-full of whiskey and the inevitable pipe.
Without a word, Wellington poured himself out a stiff dose of the spirit and gulped it down.
“You may take that pipe to the devil,” he said. His voice was quavery but determined.
“ ‘A pipe in the morning makes the sun shine,’ ” quoted Yo Len Fo.
“ ‘A pipe in the morning does not go out with the stars,’ ” replied Wellington Brown, giving proverb for proverb.

===Chinese===

威灵顿·布朗先生一天早晨醒来，觉得神清气爽，异乎寻常。往常他醒来时总是头脑昏沉、口干舌燥，心里别无所想，只求满足那一股鸦片的馋瘾——正是这瘾，害他穷了一辈子，到头来又把他的身子骨和心性一齐毁掉。可这一回，他睁开眼睛，飞快地把四周清点了一遍，发出一声嫌恶的“呸！”。他对自己的底细摸得太透，对自己的脾性、对这一阵阵缠上身来的瘾头是什么路数也太熟悉，所以他看得出：这一轮又熬到了头。总有一天，他醒来时不会再觉得神清气爽——或者干脆就醒不过来了。

他在床上坐起身，捻着胡子，把敞开的窗口吹进来的清风吸了个饱。等站到地上，他发觉两个膝盖有些发飘，便傻笑了一声。端着托盘进来的正是尤连福本人：盘上一杯清水，一瓶半满的威士忌，还有那管少不了的烟枪。

威灵顿一言不发，给自己斟了足足一盅烈酒，仰脖灌了下去。

“你把那杆烟枪拿去见鬼吧。”他说。嗓音发颤，主意却拿定了。

“‘清晨一管烟，太阳才灿烂。’”尤连福引谚答道。

“‘清晨一管烟，不随星辰熄。’”威灵顿·布朗以谚还谚。

===Original===

“If the Illustrious will stay I will have breakfast sent to him,” said the Chinaman urgently.
“I have stayed too long,” said Wellington Brown. “What is the day of the month by the foreign reckoning?”
“I do not know the foreign ways,” said Yo Len Fo, “but if your Excellency will deign to stay a few hours in this hovel﻿—”
“My Excellency will not deign to stay in any hovel or palace,” said Wellington. “Where is Yeh Ling?”
“I will send for him at once,” said the old man eagerly.
“Leave him,” replied Mr. Brown with a fine gesture and began to search his pockets. To his surprise, all his money, which was not much, was intact.

===Chinese===

“大人若肯留下，小人这就叫人送早饭来。”中国人急切地说。

“我待得够久了。”威灵顿·布朗说。“照外国的历法，今天是几月几日？”

“外国人的规矩，老朽不懂。”尤连福说，“不过大人若肯屈尊，在这寒舍再盘桓几个时辰——”

“我这位大人嘛，寒舍也罢，宫殿也罢，一概不肯屈尊。”威灵顿说。“叶凌在哪儿？”

“小人这就打发人去请。”老头儿忙不迭地说。

“不必请他。”布朗先生答道，还漂亮地一挥手，跟着便翻起自己的口袋来。叫他吃惊的是，他那一笔不算多的钱，竟原封没动。

===Original===

“How much do I owe you?” he asked.
Yo Len Fo nodded, thereby meaning “nothing.”
“Running a philanthropic hop joint?” asked the other sarcastically.
“It has all been paid by the excellent Yeh Ling,” answered the man.
Brown grunted.

===Chinese===

“我该欠你多少钱？”他问。

尤连福点了点头，意思是“分文不欠”。

“怎么，你这是开慈善烟窟来了？”对方讥诮地问。

“全都由贤明的叶凌付清了。”那人答道。

布朗哼了一声。

===Original===

“I suppose that old devil Trasmere is behind this,” he said in English, and seeing that the man did not comprehend him, he pushed his way past Yo Len Fo and went down the uncarpeted stairs into the street. He felt terribly weak, but his heart was light. Hesitating at the end of a narrow passage, he turned to the left, otherwise he could not have failed to have run into the arms of Inspector Carver who had made a call that morning upon the proprietor of the Golden Roof.
Mr. Brown’s day was spent simply. He found his way to the park and, sitting down on a bench, dozed and mused the hours away, basking in the glorious June sunlight and seemingly obvious to its heat.
Late in the afternoon he felt hungry and went to a refreshment kiosk in the park. Finishing his meal he found the nearest bench and continued his pleasant occupation of doing nothing. Mr. Wellington Brown was a born loafer; it is a knack which would prolong many lives in this strenuous age, if it could be acquired.

===Chinese===

“我料定这背后是特拉斯米尔那个老魔鬼。”他用英语说道；见那人听不明白，便从尤连福身边挤过去，走下没有铺地毯的楼梯，来到街上。他觉得自己虚弱得很，心里却是轻快的。在一条窄巷的巷口他犹豫了一下，朝左拐了——若不是这一拐，他少不得一头撞进卡弗探长的怀里：那天上午，卡弗探长刚去拜访过“金顶楼”的老板。

布朗先生这一天过得简单。他一路走到公园，在长椅上坐下，打打盹儿、出出神，把几个钟头消磨过去，晒着六月的灿烂阳光，对那一份暑气似乎浑然不觉。

傍晚时分他觉得饿了，便到公园里的小吃亭吃了顿饭。吃完，他找到最近的一张长椅，继续干那无所事事的美差。威灵顿·布朗先生天生是个闲汉；这门本事若是学得会，在这人人劳碌的年代里，不知能叫多少人延年益寿。

===Original===

The stars were coming out in a velvet blue sky when, with a shiver, he aroused himself and made instinctively for the lights. As he slouched along one of the big main paths that cross the park, he overtook a man who was walking slowly in his direction. The man shot a quick glance at him and then turned suddenly away.
“Here,” said Mr. Brown truculently, “I know you. Why in hell are you running away from me? Think I’m a leper or something?”
The man stopped, glanced uneasily left and right.
“I don’t know you,” he said coldly.
“That’s a damned lie,” snarled Brown. The reaction of his bout was upon him. He would have quarrelled with anything or anybody. “I know you and I’ve met you.” He groped in his hazy mind for some string that would lead him to the identity of the stranger. “In China, wasn’t it? My name’s Brown﻿—Wellington Brown.”
“Yes, perhaps it was in China,” said the other and of a sudden became friendly, gripped Wellington Brown’s arm and leaving the path, led him across the green spaces of the park.

===Chinese===

天鹅绒似的蓝天上，星子一颗颗亮了起来；他打了个寒噤，醒转来，凭本能朝灯火明处走去。他沿着横贯公园的一条大路懒洋洋地踱着，赶上一个朝着他这个方向慢慢走来的人。那人飞快地瞥了他一眼，忽然一下子背过身去。

“喂。”布朗先生气势汹汹地说，“我认得你。你究竟见鬼躲我干什么？当我是麻风病人还是怎么的？”

那人站住脚，不安地朝左右望了望。

“我不认识你。”他冷冷地说。

“这是天杀的谎话。”布朗咬牙切齿地说。他那一阵瘾头发作过后的躁劲正顶在身上，跟什么东西、什么人都吵得起来。“我认得你，我见过你。”他在昏蒙蒙的脑子里摸索一根线头，好牵出这陌生人的来历。“是在中国，对不对？我叫布朗——威灵顿·布朗。”

“对，也许是在中国。”对方说，忽地亲热起来，一把攥住威灵顿·布朗的胳膊，离开大路，领着他横穿过公园的草坪。

===Original===

A courting couple sitting under one of the trees saw them pass and heard Wellington Brown say:
“Don’t say that I was his storekeeper, because I wasn’t, or his servant! I was his equal, by gad. A partner in the firm, the blamed old swindler﻿—”
So they passed, the Man in Black and the besotted pensioner from China.

===Chinese===

一对坐在树底下谈情说爱的男女看见他们走过去，听见威灵顿·布朗说：

“可别说我当过他铺子里的管事，我才不是呢；也别说我是他的仆人！老天在上，我是跟他平起平坐的人，是商号里的合伙人，那该死的老骗子——”

两个身影就这样走过去了：黑衣人，和那个从中国回来的、醉生梦死的受供养者。

===Original===

At this hour another person deeply interested in Jesse Trasmere’s fate was making his final preparations for departure.
He had ventured forth in broad daylight, braved the glances of the purser of the *Arak* and had signed on as steward of the second saloon on a voyage to South Africa. The end of the long nightmare had come. Walters had to join his ship overnight, an excellent arrangement from his point of view, since it reduced the danger of detection to a minimum.
He carried with him to the big roomy docks, a respectable sum of money, the proceeds of his pilfering at Mayfield and his opportunities had been many, remembering Mr. Trasmere’s parsimony.
He had sent his bag off to the ship in the afternoon and he had only to convey himself to the docks. He went on foot, keeping to the less frequented streets, and although this entailed a longer journey he was taking no risks. A month ago he would have trembled at every shadow, and the sight of a policeman would have paralysed his activities, but now the case had been forgotten; one never read a line about it in even the more sensational newspapers, and it was with some confidence that he traversed the wharf and mounted the gangway leading to the ill-lighted decks of the liner.

===Chinese===

就在这个时辰，另一个对杰西·特拉斯米尔的下场深切关切的人，正在做出发前的最后准备。

他大着胆子在大白天出了门，硬着头皮顶住了“阿拉克号”（Arak）事务长打量的目光，签下合同，当上了二等舱的侍应生，随船跑一趟南非。这场漫长的噩梦总算到了头。沃尔特斯必须连夜上船，在他看来，这安排再好不过，因为被识破的危险由此降到了最低。

他随身带往那片宽宽敞敞的码头的，是一笔像模像样的款项——都是他在梅菲尔德小偷小摸的进项；想想特拉斯米尔先生那份吝啬，他下手的机会原也不少。

行囊他下午已经先打发上船了，剩下的只是把自己送到码头去。他步行前往，专拣行人稀少的街巷走，虽然这样要多绕些路，他却半点风险也不肯冒。换在一个月以前，他见了每一条影子都要发抖，一看见警察就会动弹不得；可如今这桩案子早叫人忘掉了，连那些最耸人听闻的报纸上，也再见不到一行相关字句。他这才怀着几分自信穿过码头，登上通往邮轮昏暗甲板的舷梯。

===Original===

“Report to the chief steward,” said the custodian on duty at the ship end of the plank and Walters enquired his way forward, went down the broad companion to the broader deck where the chief steward’s office is situated, and joined a dozen other men who were lined up in queues waiting to report.
Walters would not have worried if the waiting had occupied the rest of the evening, but in a remarkably short space of time he stepped into the chief steward’s cabin, knuckled his forehead and said:
“Reporting for duty, sir. John Williams, steward﻿—” and then he stopped.
On the further side of the steward’s table was Inspector Carver.
Walters turned in a flash but the doorway was blocked by a detective.

===Chinese===

“到侍应长那儿去报到。”在跳板靠船一端当值的看守人说。沃尔特斯一路问讯着往船头方向去，下了宽阔的扶梯，来到更宽阔的一层甲板——侍应长的办公室就设在那里——随即排进十来个列队等候报到的汉子当中。

就算这一等要把当晚余下的工夫全耗掉，沃尔特斯也不在乎；不料只过了短短一阵，他就跨进了侍应长的舱房，用指节碰了碰额头，说道：

“前来报到，长官。约翰·威廉姆斯（John Williams），侍应生——”说到这里，他停住了。

侍应长桌子的另一边，坐着卡弗探长。

沃尔特斯霍地转过身，可门口已被一名侦探堵住了。

===Original===

“All right,” he said despondently as they snapped the steel handcuffs on his wrists, “but I didn’t do it, Mr. Carver. I know nothing about the murder. I am as innocent as a babe unborn.”
“What I like about you,” said Carver unpleasantly, “is your originality.”
He followed behind the two men who held the arms of their manacled prisoner and Tab joined him. As they came off the ship, Tab asked:
“Well, do you honestly think you have him, Carver?”

===Chinese===

“好吧。”他们把钢手铐咔哒一声扣上他手腕时，他垂头丧气地说，“可那事不是我干的，卡弗先生。这桩谋杀案我一概不知。我清白得像没出娘胎的娃娃。”

“你这个人，”卡弗没好气地说，“我最欣赏的就是这份别出心裁。”

他跟在那两个汉子身后，他们一边一个架着戴铐囚犯的胳膊；塔布也凑了上来。他们走下船时，塔布问：

“怎么样，卡弗，你心里当真觉得把他拿到了吗？”

===Original===

“Who﻿—Walters? That’s the man all right, I know him very well indeed.”
“I mean the murderer,” said Tab.
“Oh, the murderer. No, I don’t think that this is the gentleman, but he will have some difficulty in proving he isn’t. You can say that he’s arrested, Tab, but I would rather you didn’t say that I charged him with the murder, because I shan’t, until I have much more information in my possession than I have at present. Perhaps if you come round to the station after you have been to the office, I will be able to tell you a little more, especially if Walters makes a statement, as I think he will.”
In this the detective was right, for Mr. Walters lost no time in putting his defence on record.

===Chinese===

“谁——沃尔特斯？这人没错儿，我实在对他知根知底。”

“我是说凶手。”塔布说。

“哦，凶手。不，我认为这位先生并不是凶手，不过他要证明自己不是，倒也得费一番周折。你可以报道说他被捕了，塔布，但我宁可你别写是我以谋杀罪控告了他，因为在我手里掌握的材料比现在多得多之前，我不会提出控告。也许你回报馆之后，到警察局来走一趟，我能多告诉你一点儿，尤其是倘若沃尔特斯肯作出一份供述——我看他是会作的。”

这一点上探长说对了，因为沃尔特斯先生一点也没耽搁，把自己的辩护词记进了笔录。

===Original===

> “My name is Walter John Felling, I have sometimes assumed the name of Walters, sometimes MacCarty. I have served three terms of imprisonment for theft and impersonation, and in July, 1913, I was sent to prison for five years at Newcastle. I was released from prison in 1917 and served in the army as a cook until 1919. On leaving the army I heard from a nose1 that Mr. Trasmere was in want of a valet, and knowing that he was a very rich man and very mean, I applied for the job, producing false references, which were made out by a man named Coleby, who does that kind of job. When Mr. Trasmere asked me what salary I wanted, I purposely said a sum which I knew was below the rate usually paid and he engaged me on the spot. I do not think he wrote for my references. If he had Coleby would have replied.
> “There were two other servants at Mayfield when I went there, a Mr. and Mrs. Green. Mr. Green was an Australian but I think Mrs. Green was born in Canada. He acted as butler to Mr. Trasmere, but he did not have a very happy time. He did not like Mr. Trasmere, I think. Certainly Mr. Trasmere did not like him. My object in securing employment with Mr. Trasmere was to find an opportunity for getting away with a good haul. I knew from the first it was going to be very difficult because of the peculiar habits of the house, but I managed to get a few things together, a gold watch and two silver candlesticks, and was thinking of making a getaway when Mr. Trasmere detected Green giving food away to Mrs. Green’s brother-in-law and fired them on the spot. Then he discovered the loss of the gold watch and had their boxes searched. I felt very sorry for Green, but of course I could say nothing.
> “After the Greens had left I had to do the work of valet and butler, too. I very soon discovered that all the valuables in the house were kept in a room in the cellar. I have never been into that room, but I know it is somewhere in the passage which leads from Mr. Trasmere’s study, because I have seen the door opened and by bending down, have been able to look along the corridor.
> “I hoped that some day or other I should be able to make a more careful inspection of the place, but that opportunity never came, although it seemed that I was going to have a chance a week or two before Mr. Trasmere’s death. I managed to get the key from his neck whilst he was in a kind of fit and take an impression, but the fit did not last very long, and I had hardly got the key back before the old gentleman recovered. It was a lucky thing for me that I had wiped the soap from the key on my sleeve, for the first thing he felt for was the chain round his neck. However, I had quite enough to work on, and I started in to make a key that would fit the impression. That is as much as I can tell you about the vault, which I never saw.
> “I went to bed every night at ten o’clock and Mr. Trasmere used to lock the door which shut me off from the rest of the house, so that it was impossible for me to see what was going on at night. I complained to him and he had a key put in a glass box in my room so that in case of emergency I could smash the glass, and with the aid of the key, unlock the door that separated me from the rest of the house. He didn’t even agree to this until he was taken ill one night and I was unable to go to his assistance.

===Chinese===

> “我叫沃尔特·约翰·费林（Walter John Felling），有时化名沃尔特斯，有时化名麦卡蒂（MacCarty）。我因盗窃和冒名顶替坐过三回牢，1913 年 7 月在纽卡斯尔被判五年监禁。1917 年出狱，在军队里当厨子，一直当到 1919 年。退伍时，我从一个眼线1那里听说，特拉斯米尔先生正要雇一名贴身男仆；我知道他是个非常有钱又非常刻薄的人，便去谋这份差事，拿出了伪造的荐书——荐书是一个名叫科尔比（Coleby）的人开的，他就专干这一行。特拉斯米尔先生问我要多少薪水时，我故意报了一个我知道低于寻常行市的数目，他当场就把我雇下了。我想他没有写信去查我的荐书。他要是写了，科尔比自会回信的。
> “我进梅菲尔德的时候，宅子里还有另外两个仆人，是格林先生和格林太太。格林先生是澳大利亚人，不过我想格林太太生在加拿大。他给特拉斯米尔先生当管家，日子过得不很舒心。我看他不喜欢特拉斯米尔先生；特拉斯米尔先生不喜欢他，那是肯定的。我谋特拉斯米尔先生手下这份差事，为的就是寻个机会大大捞上一票。打头我就知道这事非常难办，因为这户人家的习惯实在古怪；可我还是攒起了几样东西——一块金表、两支银烛台——正盘算着怎么脱身，特拉斯米尔先生偏偏撞见格林把吃食送给格林太太的姻兄，当场把他们夫妇都辞了。跟着他发觉金表丢了，叫人搜了他们的箱子。我很替格林难受，可我自然一句话也不能说。
> “格林夫妇走后，贴身男仆和管家的活计就都落到我一个人身上。我很快就发现，宅子里值钱的东西全存放在地窖的一个房间里。那个房间我从来没有进去过，但我知道它就在特拉斯米尔先生书房通出去的那条过道上的什么地方，因为我看见那扇门开过，还曾经弯下腰去，顺着走廊望进去。
> “我原指望早晚能找个机会把那地方细细察看一番，可这机会始终没来——虽说特拉斯米尔先生死前的一两个星期，我似乎要有这么一回机会。趁他犯一种昏厥似的毛病，我从他脖子上摘下钥匙，压了个印子；可那阵毛病没拖多久，我刚把钥匙送回去，老先生就缓过来了。算我走运，我早用袖子把钥匙上的肥皂擦干净了，因为他醒来头一件事，就是伸手去摸脖子上的链子。不过，我手头尽够照着干活的了，便着手配一把合那个印子的钥匙。关于那间保险库，我能奉告的就这么多——我从没见过它。
> “我每晚十点钟上床，特拉斯米尔先生总要锁上那扇把我同宅子其余部分隔开的门，叫我夜里什么动静也看不见。我向他抱怨过，他才把一把钥匙装进我房里的一只玻璃盒，这样一旦有急事，我可以砸破玻璃，用那把钥匙打开那扇把我同宅子其余部分隔开的门。就连这一点，他也是直到有天夜里病倒、我无法前去照应，才肯应承的。

===Original===

> “To open the door which locked me in was one thing, to open the little glass cupboard and take out the key was, however, a simpler matter. I used that key several times. The first time I used it, I heard voices in the dining-room downstairs and wondered who it was calling at that late hour. I hadn’t the courage to go down and see for fear I should be detected, for there was a light in the hall. But another night, hearing a woman’s voice, I went down, the lights being out, and saw a young lady sitting at a table with a typewriter in front of her, tapping the keys whilst Mr. Trasmere walked up and down, with his hands behind him, dictating. She was the prettiest young lady I have seen in my life, and somehow I was sure that I had seen her before. I did not recognize her until I saw her photograph in an illustrated paper and then it seemed to me to be impossible that it could be Miss Ursula Ardfern, the well-known actress. I came down again the next night and this time they were talking together and Mr. Trasmere called her ‘Ursula’ and I knew I was right. She used to come from the theatre every night, and sometimes he would keep her there as late as two o’clock.
> “One evening, soon after she came, I crept downstairs and in my stockinged feet, listened to them. I heard him say very sharply: ‘Ursula, where is the pin?’ The young lady answered, ‘It is there, somewhere,’ and then I heard him grumbling and grunting and presently he said, ‘Yes, here it is.’
> “There was much more to be picked up in the house than I had imagined.” (Here Walters enumerated minutely and as far as can be ascertained exactly, the number and nature of the valuables which he succeeded in acquiring.) “When Mr. Trasmere was alone he used to sit at the table with a little porcelain dish in front of him and a brush. I don’t know what he was painting, I never saw any of his pictures. I only know that he did this because I managed to peep at him on several nights, and saw him at work. He did not use a canvas, he always painted on paper, and he always used black ink. The paper must have been very thin, because once the window was slightly open and a sheet blew away.
> “I managed to see him because there was a glass fanlight over the door which I used to keep clean, and from the head of the stairs you could look into the room and if he happened to be sitting in a certain place, it was easy to see him.
> “On the morning I left the house, I was engaged in working at the key I was making, and I could do this without any danger, because Mr. Trasmere never came into my room, the door of which I kept locked in case of accidents. I served lunch to my master, and he talked to me about Brown, the man I had turned away from the door. He told me that I had done quite right and that Brown was wanted by the police in this country and he wondered why he had taken the risk of coming back. He told me that Brown was an opium taker and a drunkard, and that he was a worthless fellow. After lunch he cleared me out of the room and I knew that he was going down to his vault, which he usually did on Saturday afternoons.

===Chinese===

> “要说打开那扇把我锁在里头的门是一回事，那么打开那只小玻璃柜、取出钥匙，却是桩更容易的事。那把钥匙我使过好几回。头一回使的时候，我听见楼下饭厅里有人语声，纳闷这么晚了还有谁上门。我没胆子下去看，怕被察觉，因为门厅里亮着灯。可是另一天夜里，听见一个女人的声音，我下去了——那晚灯全灭了——看见一位年轻小姐坐在桌前，面前摆着一架打字机，手指敲着键子，特拉斯米尔先生倒背着手，在屋里踱来踱去口授。她是我这辈子见过顶标致的年轻小姐，而且不知怎么，我拿准从前见过她。直到我在一份画报上见到她的照片才认出来，当时我只觉得不可能——她竟会是那位有名的女演员乌苏拉·阿德芬小姐。第二天夜里我又下去，这一回他们俩正在说话，特拉斯米尔先生唤她‘乌苏拉’，我知道自己没认错。她每晚从剧院过来，有时他留她一直待到深夜两点钟。
> “她来了不久的一个傍晚，我蹑手蹑脚下了楼，只穿着袜子，站在那里听他们说话。我听见他非常严厉地说：‘乌苏拉，那枚别针在哪儿？’年轻小姐答道：‘在那儿呢，不知搁在哪儿了。’随后我听见他咕咕哝哝、哼哼唧唧，过了片刻说道：‘对，在这儿呢。’
> “宅子里可下手的东西，比我原先料想的多得多。”（此处沃尔特斯把他陆续到手的贵重物品，件数名目一一列举；就目前所能查证的而言，说得毫厘不爽）“特拉斯米尔先生独个儿的时候，总在桌前坐着，面前摆一只小瓷碟和一支笔。我不知道他画的是什么，他的画我一张也没见过。我只知道他确有这么一门功课，因为我好几个夜里都设法偷觑，见他伏案做活。他不用画布，总是画在纸上，用的总是黑墨。那纸必定非常薄，因为有一回窗户开了一道缝，一张纸竟叫风刮跑了。
> “我所以能看见他，是因为那扇门头上有一扇玻璃亮窗，素来由我擦得干干净净；从楼梯顶上望过去，正好瞧得进屋——他若凑巧坐在某个座位上，一眼便能望见他。
> “我离开那宅子的当天上午，我正埋头做那把配着的钥匙；这活儿干起来毫无风险，因为特拉斯米尔先生从不进我的房间，那扇门我总是锁着，以防万一。我伺候主人吃午饭，他同我谈起布朗，就是叫我从门口打发走的那个。他说我做得一点不错，还说布朗在本国正被警察通缉，他不懂他干吗要冒这个险回来。他告诉我，布朗是个抽鸦片的，又是个酒鬼，是个不成器的家伙。午饭后他把我支出屋去，我知道他要下他的保险库去了——礼拜六下午他照例都去。

===Original===

> “At about ten minutes to three I was in my room working at the key and had just brought a cup of coffee from the kitchen, when the front door bell rang and I answered it. There was a messenger boy with a telegram and it was addressed to me. I had never before received telegrams at the house and I was surprised. On opening it I read a message reminding me that I had been convicted at Newcastle eight years before and telling me that the police were calling at three o’clock.
> “I was in a terrible state of mind, for I had in my room a considerable quantity of stolen property and I knew that my next conviction would mean a very long sentence. I rushed up to the room, gathered my stuff together and was out of the house a little before three. As I opened the door I saw Mr. Rex Lander standing by the gate. I had seen Mr. Lander before, because he had stayed for a little time in the house a month after I had taken up my position. He had always been very nice to me and he is a gentleman for whom I have a great deal of respect.
> “His uncle, the late Mr. Trasmere, did not like him. He told me once that Mr. Rex was extravagant and lazy. On seeing Mr. Rex at the gate my heart went down into my boots and I thought that he must immediately detect that something was wrong. He asked me if his uncle was ill and that gave me a moment to pull myself together, and I told him that I was going on a very urgent errand and running into the street, I had the good luck to find a taxicab which drove me to the Central Station. I did not, however, leave town, but made my way to a room which I had once occupied in a house which I knew in Reed Street, where I have been in hiding ever since. I did not see Mr. Trasmere again after lunch. He did not come out to enquire who had called when the telegram arrived; there were frequent callers, tradesmen and others, and I never reported to him unless there was something important or unless letters or telegrams came for him. I have never been in the vault or in the passage leading to the vault, nor have I at any time owned a revolver.
> “I make this statement voluntarily, without any pressure, and have answered the questions which Inspector Carver has put to me, without any suggestion on his part as to the way they should be answered.”

===Chinese===

> “两点五十来分钟，我正在房里做那把钥匙，刚从厨房端了一杯咖啡，前门门铃响了，我去开了门。是个送电报的信童，电报竟是写给我的。我在这宅子里从来不曾收到过电报，不由得诧异。拆开一读，那电文提醒我八年前在纽卡斯尔被定过罪，又说警察三点钟就要登门。
