The present which Rex had bought “in Rome” for his friend had been purchased within a few miles of Doughty Street and was intended to emphasise the fact that Rex was abroad.
It was nearing twelve o’clock when a phone message came through from police headquarters, and Carver went to the instrument.

===Chinese===

“有一张巴比奇（Burbidges）开来的蓝宝石戒指付讫账单。”那人说。卡弗淡淡一笑。
雷克斯那件自称“在罗马”买给朋友的礼物，其实是在离道蒂街不出几英里的地方买的，为的是坐实他身在国外这件事。
将近十二点时，警察总部来了电话，卡弗走到话机跟前。

===Original===

“Is that you, Mr. Carver﻿—Mayfield is burning﻿—the brigade have just had a call.”
Carver dropped the receiver as if it were red-hot and flew to the door. A cab had just set down some guests at the hotel and he pushed unceremoniously past them.
“Peak Avenue,” he said.
What a fool not to have thought of Mayfield before! He cursed volubly in the darkness of the taxi. And after he knew that Rex Lander had called Tab on the phone, and that Tab had gone out! Of course, that was where he would have taken Tab﻿—to Mayfield. Tab would have gone cheerfully, having no suspicion of his friend, and﻿—Carver shuddered.
He had read only too clearly the significance of the torn photographs. The man was insanely jealous; would stop at nothing. With two murders to his credit, a third would be simple.

===Chinese===

“是卡弗先生吗——梅菲尔德烧起来了——消防队刚接到报警。”
卡弗扔下听筒，仿佛那东西烧得通红，飞奔出门。一辆出租汽车刚在饭店门前放下几位客人，他顾不得礼数，从他们中间硬挤了过去。
“去皮克大街。”他说。
怎么就没先想到梅菲尔德，多蠢啊！在出租汽车的黑暗里，他把自己痛骂了个够。更何况他已经知道雷克斯·兰德给塔布打过电话、塔布已经出了门！那家伙当然会把塔布带到那儿去——带到梅菲尔德。塔布对朋友毫无疑心，必定欢欢喜喜地跟了去，于是——卡弗浑身一颤。
那些被撕碎的照片意味着什么，他早已看得再透彻不过。这个人妒火中烧，近乎疯狂；什么事都干得出来。手上已经背着两条人命，再添第三条不过是举手之劳。

===Original===

Long before he reached Peak Avenue, he saw the red glow in the sky and groaned. Amidst that blazing hell Rex Lander had destroyed not only his rival but half the evidence of his crime.
The cab dashed through the police line into the Avenue, crowded now with half-dressed inhabitants, and brightly lit by the flames that mounted above the doomed house. The roof fell in as he sprang from the cab; a crowd of sparks leapt into the dark sky and Carver could only stand speechless and sorrowful beyond expression.
Then it was that somebody tapped his elbow, and looking round he saw a man in a soaked and bedraggled dressing-gown. At first he did not recognise him, for the little man’s face was blackened and scorched, his eyes were red and wild.
“My father was a fireman,” said Mr. Stott solemnly. “We Stotts are a hard bit’n race. Heroes all of us!”
Carver looked at the man aghast.
Mr. Stott was very drunk!

===Chinese===

还没到皮克大街，他远远就望见天边的红光，不由呻吟出声。在那片烈焰地狱之中，雷克斯·兰德毁掉的不只是他的情敌，还有他罪行的半数罪证。
出租汽车风驰电掣般冲过警戒线，驶入大街。街上此刻挤满了衣衫不整的居民，火焰从那座注定焚毁的宅子上空腾起，照得四下通明。他跳下车时，屋顶正塌陷下去；一大团火星蹿入黑暗的夜空。卡弗只能站在那里，一言不发，悲恸得无以言表。
就在这时，有人在他胳膊肘上轻轻一碰。回头一看，是个穿着湿透的、邋里邋遢晨衣的男人。起初他没认出是谁——这小个子男人满脸乌黑、燎得焦糊，两眼通红，狂乱无神。
“我父亲就是干救火这一行的。”斯托特先生一本正经地说，“我们斯托特家的人天生就是硬骨头族类。个个都是英雄！”
卡弗惊骇地望着这个人。
斯托特先生早已烂醉如泥！

## XXXIX / 三十九

===Original===

Yeh Ling wrote:

> “Dear Miss Ardfern: I am giving what you call a housewarming on Monday next. Will you not come? And please if you can, will you persuade Mr. Carver and Mr. Holland also to be my guests for this festivity?”

===Chinese===

叶凌写道：

> “亲爱的阿德芬小姐：下周一，我将举办一场你们所说的‘乔迁庆宴’。您肯赏光吗？另外，如果办得到，可否请您也说服卡弗先生和荷兰先生，一同来做这场喜庆的宾客？”

===Original===

The girl wrote instantly accepting the invitation both on her own and Tab’s behalf.

“It is a great idea,” said the news editor, “there is a story in that house, Tab. Now, boy, see if for once in your young life you can turn in a really informative column! There is something gone wrong with your stuff lately, the night editors are complaining bitterly about the slush that finds its way into your literary efforts. You are not supposed to refer to the Secretary of State as ‘darling,’ and it is not usual to speak of a judge as ‘beloved.’ ”

===Chinese===

姑娘立即回信，既代表她自己、也代表塔布接受了邀请。

“这主意妙极了，”新闻主编说，“那座宅子里就藏着一篇新闻呢，塔布。行了小伙子，看看你这辈子能不能破天荒交出一篇真正有料的专栏！你近来写的玩意儿有点不对劲，夜班编辑们正叫苦连天，抱怨那些混进你生花妙笔里的无病呻吟的滥调。你总不该管国务大臣叫‘达令’，也不兴管一位法官叫‘敬爱的’吧。”

===Original===

Tab went very red.

“Do I do that, Jacques?” he asked conscience-stricken.

“You do worse than that,” said Jacques. “Now﻿—a good story about those pillars of Yeh Ling’s. Get a touch of the flaming east into your mundane exercises, will you?”

Tab promised faithfully that he would.

===Chinese===

塔布的脸腾地红了。

“我真这么写了，雅克？”他良心不安地问。

“你干得比这还要糟，”雅克说，“这样吧——去写一篇关于叶凌那些柱子的好报道。给你那些平淡无奇的功课里添一点炽烈的东方色彩，办得到吗？”

塔布信誓旦旦地答应了下来。

===Original===

He had the unexpected pleasure of meeting Mr. Stott at the housewarming, and introducing that gentleman to Ursula. Mr. Stott had a particular interest in Yeh Ling’s fabric, for, as he explained some dozen times, he had put in the foundations.

“I owe you a very great deal, Mr. Stott,” said Ursula warmly. “Tab﻿—Mr. Holland has told me how splendidly brave you were on the night of the fire.”

Mr. Stott coughed.

“There is some talk in town of presenting me with a piece of plate,” he said deprecatingly, “I have done my best to stop it. I hate a fuss about a trifle of that description. The curious thing is, all my family have disliked that kind of fuss. Our family has always hated publicity. My father, who was perhaps the best minister in the Baptist movement, might have gone into the church and become a bishop﻿—in fact, they practically offered him a bishopric﻿—he was just the same. I remember﻿—”

===Chinese===

在这场乔迁庆宴上，他意外地喜逢斯托特先生，并把这位先生介绍给了乌苏拉。斯托特先生对叶凌宅邸的建筑格外关切，因为——正如他翻来覆去解释了十几遍的——这宅子的地基是他亲手打的。

“我真是欠了您天大的人情，斯托特先生，”乌苏拉热忱地说，“塔布——荷兰先生已经跟我说过，起火那天夜里您是多么英勇无畏。”

斯托特先生咳了一声。

“城里正有人张罗着要赠我一件纪念银器，”他谦抑地说，“我已尽力把这事拦下。我最讨厌为这么点小事大肆张罗。说来也怪，我们全家历来都不喜欢这一套热闹。我们这一族一向厌恶抛头露面。我父亲——他或许是浸礼会运动中最出色的一位牧师——当年满可以进国教会当上主教，事实上，人家差不多就要奉上一顶主教冠了——他就是这么个脾气。我记得——”

===Original===

Yeh Ling led them through the house, showing them his art treasures accumulated with some labour, and now seeing the light of day for the first time.

Ursula felt very happy, was childishly appreciative and enthusiastic over every beautiful little statuette, over every example of the naive painters’ art which Yeh Ling showed her.

===Chinese===

叶凌领着他们把宅子走了个遍，向他们展示自己颇费了一番心血搜罗来的艺术珍藏——这些宝贝如今才头一回重见天日。

乌苏拉心里非常快活，带着孩子气的欣赏与热忱，叶凌展示给她的每一尊精美小雕像、每一件质朴派画家的作品，都令她赞叹不已。

===Original===

“Yeh Ling,” she said, when they were alone for a second, “have you heard any news of Mr. Lander?”

He shook his head.

“Do you think he has got away to another country?” she asked.

“I think not,” said Yeh Ling.

“Do you know, Yeh Ling?” she said meaningly.

“I can only assure you, Miss Ardfern,” said Yeh Ling, waving the cool air into his face with a beautifully painted fan, “that I have never looked upon Mr. Lander’s face since the night I saw him at the Golden Roof.”

===Chinese===

“叶凌，”趁旁人不在跟前的一瞬，她开了口，“你有兰德先生的消息吗？”

他摇了摇头。

“你想，他是不是逃到外国去了？”她问。

“我想没有。”叶凌说。

“你知不知道呢，叶凌？”她意味深长地问。

“我只能向您保证，阿德芬小姐，”叶凌说着，用一柄画得极美的扇子把凉风扇向自己的脸，“自那晚在金顶楼见过兰德先生一面之后，我再也没有见过他。”

===Original===

She was content with this, but﻿—

“Who was Wellington Brown?” she asked in a strained voice.

“Lady,” said Yeh Ling gently, “he is dead: it was better that he died so than in the way you feared.”

She passed her hand before her eyes and nodded.

“We Chinese forgive our fathers much,” said Yeh Ling, and left her to her grief.

===Chinese===

这个答复让她放了心，只是——

“威灵顿·布朗是谁？”她声音发紧地问。

“小姐，”叶凌温和地说，“他死了：他这样死去，总好过您先前害怕的那种死法。”

她抬手在眼前拂了一下，点了点头。

“我们中国人，对父辈总是多有宽恕的。”叶凌说罢，把她独自留在她的哀伤里。

===Original===

From the house he took his guests to the terrace gardens, and then down the broad yellow avenue to the two massive grey pillars that stood guard at the entrance of his domain.

“You had a lot of trouble with these, I am sure,” said Stott, casting a professional eye upward.

“With only one,” said Yeh Ling, and his fan moved to and fro languidly. “With the Pillar of Grateful Memories there was a hitch. Somebody came into the ground one night whilst it was raining and let cement into the mould, cut off the hauling rope and did other trivial damage. My builder thought that the pillar would not set, but it has.”

===Chinese===

出了宅子，他领客人们走进台地花园，随后沿一条宽阔的黄色大道走下去，来到把守在他领地入口的那两根灰色巨柱跟前。

“这两根柱子，您一定费了不少周折吧。”斯托特说着，以内行的眼光朝上端详。

“只有一根费了点事，”叶凌说，手里的扇子懒懒地来回摇着。“感戴记忆之柱出过一桩小岔子。有一天夜里正下着雨，有人潜进场地，往模子里灌了水泥，割断了吊装的缆绳，还干了些别的无关痛痒的破坏。我的营造商原以为这柱子凝不成形，结果还是凝成了。”

===Original===

He looked up at the smooth face of the concrete and his eyes rested some dozen feet above the ground.

“I have dedicated this to all who have helped me, to the old man Shi Soh, to you, Miss Ardfern﻿—to all gods western and eastern, to all who love and are loved.”

===Chinese===

他抬眼望着混凝土光洁的柱面，目光停在离地十几英尺的高处。

“我把它献给了所有帮助过我的人，献给了老人石索，献给了您，阿德芬小姐——献给西方与东方的一切神明，献给一切相爱着与被深爱着的人。”

===Original===

When his guests had gone, Yeh Ling, in his blue and gold satin dress of ceremony, came back to the pillar, and there was a little book in his hand. His finger was inserted midway.

The servant who accompained him he dismissed.

“I believe,” said Yeh Ling, “I shall be happier﻿—” He stood facing the pillar, bowed, then opening the book, he began to read in his deep rich voice. He was reading the service for the burial of the dead.

===Chinese===

客人散尽之后，叶凌身穿那身蓝底金花的缎面礼服，回到了柱子跟前；他手里托着一本小书，手指夹在书页半中央。

随他同来的那个仆人，被他打发走了。

“我相信，”叶凌说，“我往后会更安心些——”他面朝柱子站定，躬身行了一礼，随后翻开那本书，用深沉圆润的嗓音诵读起来。他诵的，是安葬亡者的祷文。

===Original===

When he had finished he lit three joss sticks which stood in the blue vase the servant had carried, and placed them before the pillar, kowtowing deeply. Then from his capacious sleeve he produced some strips of gold paper suitably inscribed, and these he burnt.

“I think those are all the gods I know,” said Yeh Ling, dusting his fingers daintily.

===Chinese===

诵毕，他点燃三炷香——香就插在仆人先前捧来的那只蓝花瓶里——将它们供到柱前，又深深地叩下头去。随后，他从宽大的衣袖中取出几条写好了字的金纸，把它们付之一炬。

“我想，我认得的神明，都在这儿了。”叶凌说着，斯斯文文地掸了掸手指。

## XXXV / 三十五

===Original===

Eline Simpson with a large handkerchief tied round her face, turned on her bed and groaned. It was unfortunate for all concerned that Eline’s bedroom was immediately above that occupied by Mr. John Stott and his wife, although Eline’s groans had no serious effect upon that lady.
Mr. Stott had reached the stage where he waited with agonized expectancy for the next boom of anguish; when it did not come he was frantic, when it finally shivered the walls of his room, he was maddened. Eline was an irregular groaner.
“Eline goes tomorrow!” he roared, and even Mrs. Stott heard him.
“She’s had her tooth out,” said Mrs. Stott sleepily.

===Chinese===

艾琳·辛普森（Eline Simpson）脸上扎着一块大手帕，在床上翻了个身，呻吟起来。艾琳的卧室恰在约翰·斯托特先生伉俪卧室的正上方，这对所有相关的人来说都是桩不幸——虽说艾琳的呻吟对那位太太并未造成什么大碍。
斯托特先生已到了这般田地：苦巴巴地干等着下一声哀号炸响；等不来，他抓心挠肝；等它终于震得他房间的四壁发颤，他又几乎要发狂。艾琳呻吟起来没个准点儿。
“艾琳明天就走！”他吼道，连斯托特太太都被他吵醒了。
“她拔过牙了。”斯托特太太睡意惺忪地说。

===Original===

“Go upstairs and tell that girl to get up and walk about﻿—no, no, not to walk about, to sit still.”
“M’m,” said Mrs. Stott, and sighed happily.
Mr. Stott glared at her and then came another groan from above. He got out of bed and into his dressing-gown﻿—it was really a kimono﻿—and trotted up the stairs.
“Eline!” he called in a hushed intense voice, suitable to the hour and the occasion.

===Chinese===

“上楼去叫那丫头起来走动走动——不，不，不是走动走动，是安坐不动。”
“唔。”斯托特太太应了一声，幸福地叹了口气。
斯托特先生怒瞪她一眼，这时头顶上又传来一声呻吟。他下了床，套上晨衣——其实是件和服——蹬蹬蹬小跑上楼去了。
“艾琳！”他压着嗓子急急唤道，声口正配这个时辰、这场合。

===Original===

“Yes, sir,” pathetically.
“What the he﻿—why are you making such a﻿—such a hullabaloo?”
“Oh, my tooth does ache, sir!” she wailed jerkily.
“Nonsense!” said Mr. Scott, “how can it ache when it is at the dentist’s? Don’t be a baby. Get up and take something, come downstairs, dress yourself decently,” he warned her.

===Chinese===

“在，先生。”她可怜巴巴地应道。
“搞什么名——你干吗弄出这样的——这样的大呼小叫？”
“哎哟，我的牙疼得厉害，先生！”她一顿一顿地哀声说。
“胡说！”斯托特先生说，“牙都留在牙医那儿了，还怎么疼？别小孩子气。起来吃点东西，下楼来，穿得像样点儿。”他告诫她道。

===Original===

He went down into the dining-room and from a secret cupboard produced a bottle with a boastful label. Into a tumbler he splashed a very generous portion.
Eline came in a flannel dressing-gown and skirt. She looked scarcely human.
“Drink this,” commanded Mr. Stott.
Eline took the glass timidly and examined it.

===Chinese===

他下楼进了餐室，从一只暗柜里取出一瓶标签吹得天花乱坠的酒，往一只平底大杯里哗啦斟上慷慨得很的一份。
艾琳穿着法兰绒晨衣、罩着裙子进来了。她看上去几乎没了人样。
“把这个喝了。”斯托特先生命令道。
艾琳怯生生接过杯子，端详着它。

===Original===

“I could never drink that, sir,” she said, awestricken.
“Drink it!” commanded Mr. Stott fiercely, “it is nothing.”
To prove that it was nothing, he poured himself out an even more impressive quantity and tossed it down. In retaliation, the whiskey almost tossed down Mr. Stott. At any rate, he staggered under the shock. Fortunately for his reputation as a hard and easy drinker, Eline was oblivious to everything except a sense of complete suffocation accompanied by a feeling that she had swallowed a large ladleful of molten lead. So she did not see Mr. Stott gasping like a fish and clutching his throat.
“Oh, sir﻿—what was it?” she found voice to ask.

===Chinese===

“这个我可怎么也喝不下去，先生。”她说，一脸敬畏。
“喝了它！”斯托特先生厉声命令，“这不算什么。”
为了证明这不算什么，他给自己斟上分量更叫人叹服的一杯，一仰脖灌了下去。作为回敬，威士忌险些把斯托特先生撂倒。不管怎么说，他在这冲击之下打了个趔趄。幸而他素来顶着个酒量又硬又随和的名声，而艾琳此刻满心只有一股彻底的噎塞憋闷，外加仿佛吞下了一大勺熔化铅水的感觉，此外一概不知。于是她没有看见斯托特先生正像条离水的鱼似的张嘴喘气，一手掐着自己的喉咙。
“哎呀，先生——那是什么？”她好不容易才出声问道。

===Original===

“Whiskey,” said Mr. Stott in a strangled voice, “neat whiskey! It is nothing.”
Eline had never drunk neat whiskey before. It seemed to her, as whiskey, distinctly untidy. It had sharp edges. She could only look upon her employer with a new born respect.
“It is nothing,” said Mr. Stott again. Now that it was all over it seemed at any rate, easy. He was an abstemious man and in truth had never tasted whiskey in its undiluted state. Bravado had made him do it, but now that it was done, he had no regrets.
“How’s your tooth?”

===Chinese===

“威士忌，”斯托特先生声音哽塞地说，“纯威士忌！不算什么。”
艾琳从前从来没有喝过纯威士忌。在她看来，这威士忌作为一种酒，实在散漫得可以，浑身是锋利的棱角。她只能怀着一种新生的敬意看待自己的东家。
“不算什么。”斯托特先生又说了一遍。事到如今，这一手至少显得容易了。他是个饮食有节的人，说实在的，从来没尝过不掺水的威士忌。是一时逞勇让他喝的，可如今喝也喝了，他毫无悔意。
“你的牙怎么样了？”

===Original===

“Fine, sir,” said Eline gratefully. She experienced a wonderful sense of exhilaration. So did Mr. Stott.
“Sit down, Eline,” he pointed grandly to a chair.
Eline smiled foolishly and sat.
“I have always been a very heavy drinker,” said Mr. Stott gravely. “My father was before me. I am what is known as a three-bottle man.”

===Chinese===

“好啦，先生。”艾琳感激地说。她体验到一种妙不可言的振奋。斯托特先生也是如此。
“坐下，艾琳。”他气派十足地指了指一把椅子。
艾琳傻乎乎地笑着坐下了。
“我一向是个酒量极重的人，”斯托特先生一本正经地说，“我父亲在我之前就是。我是人们所说的那种‘三瓶汉子’。”

===Original===

He wondered at himself as he spoke. His maligned parent had been a Baptist minister.
“Goodness!” said Eline impressed, “and there are only two bottles on the sideboard!”
Mr. Stott looked.
“There is only one, Eline,” he said severely, and looked again. “Yes, perhaps you’re right.” He closed first one eye and then the other. “Only one,” he said.

===Chinese===

说这话时他心里也暗自纳罕。他那位被糟蹋的老父，其实是个浸礼会牧师。
“天哪！”艾琳肃然起敬，“可餐具柜上明明只摆着两个瓶子呀！”
斯托特先生看了看。
“只有一个，艾琳。”他板着脸说，又看了一遍。“嗯，也许你是对的。”他先闭上一只眼，又闭上另一只。“只有一个。”他说。

===Original===

“Two,” murmured Eline defiantly.
“We Stotts have always been devil-may-care fellows,” said Mr. Stott moodily. “Into one scrape and out of another. Hard drinking, hard riding, hard living men, the salt of the earth, Eline.”
“There are three bottles!” said Eline in wonderment.
“My father fought Kid McGinty for twenty-five rounds.” Mt. Stott shook his head. “And beat him to﻿—to﻿—a jelly. Hard fighters every one of us. By heaven,” he said, his pugilistic mood reviving certain memories, “if I had laid my hands on that scoun’rel﻿ ﻿… !”

===Chinese===

“两个。”艾琳不服气地嘟囔。
“我们斯托特家的人，历来都是天不怕地不怕的汉子，”斯托特先生郁郁地说，“才出一桩乱子，又陷进另一桩。狂饮、狂骑、玩命过活的人——世间的盐，艾琳。”
“有三个瓶子！”艾琳惊叹道。
“我父亲跟基德·麦金蒂（Kid McGinty）打了二十五个回合。”斯托特先生摇了摇头。“把他打得——打得——成了一摊肉酱。我们家的个顶个都是硬拳手。皇天在上，”他说，拳击的兴致勾起了某些旧事，“要是让我落到那个恶棍手里……！”

===Original===

He walked heavily, rose and walked with long strides into the hall. Eline scenting action, followed. Her strides were not so long, but longer than she expected. Mr. Stott was standing on the doorstep, his hands on his hip, his legs apart, and he was looking disparagingly at Mayfield.
“Come any more of your tricks﻿—and look out!” he challenged. “You’ll find a Stott﻿—”
Eline clutched his arm frenziedly.
“Oh sir﻿—there’s somebody there!”

===Chinese===

他沉重地站起身，迈开大步走进门厅。艾琳嗅出要出事，跟了上去。她的步子没有那样大，走的路却比她料想的长。斯托特先生站在门阶上，双手叉腰，两腿分开，正一脸轻蔑地望着梅菲尔德。
“再敢耍一回花招——给我放明白点！”他叫阵道，“叫你们领教领教斯托特家的——”
艾琳发疯似的攥住他的胳膊。
“哎呀先生——那里头有人！”

===Original===

Undoubtedly there was somebody there; a light was showing in the front room, a red and uncertain light. And then a door closed loudly.
“Somebody there﻿—?”
Mr. Stott strode down the steps furiously. Even when he strode down a step that wasn’t there, he did not lose his poise.
“Somebody there﻿—?”

===Chinese===

那里头无疑是有人；前屋里亮着灯，一种红惨惨、晃悠悠的光。接着，一扇门砰的一声关上了。
“有人在里头——？”
斯托特先生怒冲冲地大步跨下台阶。即便一脚踏空、迈下一级并不存在的台阶，他也没有失了气度。
“有人在里头——？”

===Original===

He remembered mistily that the gardener had a lazy habit of leaving his spade beneath the trimmed hedge that marked the boundaries of his property.
“You’ll catch your death of cold, dear,” wailed Eline outrageously.
But Mr. Stott neither observed the uncalled-for endearment, nor the rain that soaked him, nor the wind that flapped his dressing-gown loose. He groped for the spade and found it, just as a car came smashing through the frail gate of Mayfield.
“Hi, you, sir!” shouted Mr. Stott fiercely, “what in hell do you mean, sir!”

===Chinese===

他迷迷糊糊记起，园丁有个偷懒的习惯，总把铁锹撂在修剪齐整的树篱底下——那树篱标着他家地界的边沿。
“亲爱的，您这样要冻出人命来的呀！”艾琳没轻没重地哀声叫道。
可斯托特先生既没理会那声无端的亲昵，也没理会浇透他的雨水、吹得他晨衣敞开翻飞的风。他摸索着找那把铁锹，刚摸到手，一辆汽车便撞破梅菲尔德那扇单薄的栅门冲了进来。
“嗨，说你呢，先生！”斯托特先生厉声喝道，“你他妈的安的什么心，先生！”

===Original===

He stood in the centre of the road, brandishing his spade﻿—the mudguard of the car just missed him.
Mr. Stott turned and stared after.
“Disgusting﻿—no lights!” he said.
But there were lights in Mayfield, white and red and yellow lights, that flickered up in long caressing tongues.

===Chinese===

他站在大路当中，挥舞着铁锹——汽车的挡泥板擦着他飞了过去。
斯托特先生转过身，直勾勾望着车去的方向。
“不像话——连个灯都不开！”他说。
可梅菲尔德里亮着灯，白的、红的、黄的灯，长长的火舌爱抚似的舔了上来，忽明忽暗。

===Original===

“Fire!” said Mr. Stott thickly.
He staggered up to the door of Mayfield and brought his spade down upon the narrow glass panel with a crash. Putting in his hand, he found the knob of the door and fell into the passage.
“Fire!” boomed Mr. Stott.
He had an idea that something ought to be done﻿—a feeling that somebody should be rescued. The dining-room was blazing at the window end and by the light he saw an open door. Below was a glow of steady illumination.

===Chinese===

“着火啦！”斯托特先生口齿含混地说。
他踉跄着扑到梅菲尔德门前，抡起铁锹，哗啦一声砸在窄窄的玻璃门格上。他把手从破洞里伸进去，摸到门把，一头栽进了走廊。
“着火啦！”斯托特先生瓮声瓮气地吼道。
他有个念头，觉得总该做点什么——总觉得有谁该去搭救。餐室靠窗那一头已是烈焰熊熊，借着火光他看见一扇敞开的门。门底下透出一片安稳的光亮。

===Original===

“Anybody there?” shouted Mr. Stott.
And then a shiver ran down his spine for a distant voice called:
“Here!”
“Fire!” yelled Mr. Stott, and stumbled down the steps. The voice came from a door.

===Chinese===

“有人在吗？”斯托特先生喊道。
就在这时，一个遥远的声音应了一句：“在这儿！”他顿时脊背上一凉。
“在这儿！”
“着火啦！”斯托特先生尖声喊道，跌跌撞撞跨下台阶。声音是从一扇门里传出来的。

===Original===

“Wait, I’ll kick out the key.”﻿ ﻿…
There was a sound of metallic scraping and something hit the brickwork at his feet.
Mr. Stott frowned at it. A key.
“Open the door,” said the voice urgently.

===Chinese===

“等等，我把钥匙踢出去。”……
一阵金属刮擦声响过，有什么东西打在他脚边的砖地上。
斯托特先生对着它皱起了眉。一把钥匙。
“开门。”那声音急促地说。

===Original===

Mr. Stott stooped and picked it up, made three shots at the keyhole and at last got it.
A man doubled up as if in pain, shuffled out.
“Unfasten the strap,” he commanded.
“There’s a fire,” said Mr. Stott impressively.

===Chinese===

斯托特先生弯腰拾起钥匙，对着锁眼比划了三下才终于捅了进去。
一个男人弓着身子，好像疼得厉害，蹭着碎步出来了。
“解开皮带。”他命令道。
“这儿着着火呢。”斯托特先生郑重其事地说。

===Original===

“So I observe, quick!”
Stott unbuckled the strap and the man stood up.
“Get those papers﻿—on the table,” said the strange man. “I can’t touch them, I’m handcuffed behind.”
The rescuer obeyed.

===Chinese===

“我看得出来。快点！”
斯托特解开了皮带扣，那人站直了身子。
“把那些文件拿上——桌上。”那陌生的男人说，“我自己拿不了，我两只手反铐在背后。”
这位救命恩人从命照办。

===Original===

The passage was thick with smoke and suddenly all lights went out.
“Now run!” hissed Tab and Mr. Stott, still gripping his spade, groped forward. At the foot of the steps he paused. The heat was fierce, the flames were curling down over the top step.
“Whack the floor﻿—the carpet with your spade and run﻿—don’t worry about me!”
Mr. Stott made a wild rush up the stairs, striking more wildly at the floor. The smoke blinded him; he was scorched, he felt his few locks shrivel in the heat.

===Chinese===

走廊里浓烟滚滚，忽然之间，所有的灯都灭了。
“快跑！”塔布嘶声低喝。斯托特先生仍旧攥着铁锹，摸索着向前。到了台阶脚下，他停住了。热浪灼人，火舌已经卷过顶上那级台阶。
“用铁锹抽地板——抽那地毯——然后就跑——别管我！”
斯托特先生发疯似的往楼梯上冲，抡起铁锹更发疯似的抽打地板。浓烟迷了他的眼；他被灼烤着，只觉自己那几根头发在热浪里蜷缩焦卷。

===Original===

And then Tab Holland behind pushed him with his shoulder and it seemed to Mr. Stott that he was being thrown into the fiery furnace. He uttered one yell and leapt. In a fraction of a second he was in the passage﻿—gasping and alive.
“Outside﻿ ﻿… !”
Tab thrust his shoulder again at the dazed man and Mr. Stott walked out into the rain just as the first fire engine came clanging into the street.
“There *is* a fire,” said Mr. Stott with satisfaction. “Come and have a drink.”

===Chinese===

接着，塔布·荷兰从背后用肩膀一拱，斯托特先生只觉得自己被扔进了烈火的窑里。他怪叫一声，纵身一跃。眨眼之间，他已置身走廊——喘着粗气，人还活着。
“外头……！”
塔布又用肩膀推了推这位晕头转向的人，斯托特先生便走进了雨里，恰在此时，第一辆消防车当啷当啷驶进街来。
“这可是真着了火，”斯托特先生心满意足地说，“来喝一杯。”

===Original===

Tab wanted something more than a drink. He saw a running policeman and hailed him.
“Officer, can you unlock these handcuffs? I’m Holland of the *Megaphone*. Good business!”
A turn of the key and he was free.
He stretched his aching arms.

===Chinese===

塔布想要的不止是一杯酒。他望见一个跑步而来的警察，招呼住他。
“警官，能帮忙打开这副手铐吗？我是《扩音器报》的荷兰。痛快！”
钥匙一转，他就自由了。
他舒展开两条酸痛的胳膊。

===Original===

“Com’n have a drink,” urged Mr. Stott, and Tab thought that the suggestion was not altogether foolish.
They came to Mr. Stott’s dining-room to find Eline singing in a high falsetto voice, a voice which had aroused even Mrs. Stott, for that good lady, in deshabille, was regarding the musical Eline with wonder and shame when they arrived.
The good lady staggered at the appearance of her husband. Tab seemed a less notable phenomenon﻿—even the vocal Eline faded from the picture.
“What is the meaning of this?” she asked tearfully.

===Chinese===

“来嘛，喝一杯。”斯托特先生怂恿道，塔布觉得这提议倒也不算全然糊涂。
两人来到斯托特先生的餐室，只见艾琳正扯着高亢的假嗓子唱歌——这嗓子连斯托特太太都被惊动了，因为他们到时，那位好太太衣衫不整，正又惊又臊地打量着献歌的艾琳。
一见丈夫的模样，好太太摇晃了一下。塔布似乎算不上多么扎眼的奇观——连放歌的艾琳也从这幅画面里黯淡了下去。
“这是怎么一回事？”她含泪问道。

===Original===

“There’s been a fire,” murmured her husband.
He glared at Eline fiendishly and pointed to the door.
“Shut up, girl! Go to bed! You’re fired﻿—you’re the secon’ fire tonight!”
He was so overcome by his witticism that he relapsed into what promised to be continuous laughter. The clang of another engine arrested his merriment, and he stalked out of the house.

===Chinese===

“着了一场火。”她丈夫喃喃道。
他恶魔似的瞪了艾琳一眼，一指房门。
“闭嘴，丫头！回床上去！你被炒了——你是今晚的第二把‘火’！”
他被自己的妙语折服得不行，陷入眼看要笑个不停的境地。又一辆消防车当啷当啷驶来，截住了他的欢畅，他昂首阔步走出了宅子。

===Original===

“I don’t think Mr. Stott is quite well,” said Mrs. Stott in a tremulous voice. “I﻿—be quiet, Eline! Singing sacred songs at this hour of the morning!”
And then came Mr. Stott in a hurry, and behind him, Carver.
“Thank God, my boy﻿—I never expected﻿—!”
Carver found a difficulty in speaking.

===Chinese===

“我看斯托特先生是有点不大舒服。”斯托特太太声音发颤地说，“我——安静点，艾琳！一大早这个钟点，竟唱起圣歌来！”
接着斯托特先生急匆匆地进来了，身后跟着卡弗。
“感谢上帝，我的孩子——我万万没有料到——！”
卡弗觉得开口都吃力。

===Original===

“I rescued ’m,” said Mr. Stott loudly.
His face was black, what of his dressing-gown was not singed, was sodden. He flourished the spade.
“I rescued ’m,” said Mr. Stott with dignity. “We Stotts come of a hard bit’n race. My father was a firem’n﻿—he rescued thousan’s from burnin’.”
Here he was getting near to the truth, for, as had been before remarked, Mr. Stott’s father was a Baptist minister.

===Chinese===

“是我救的他。”斯托特先生大声说。
他满脸乌黑，晨衣上没烧焦的地方也湿透了。他挥舞着那把铁锹。
“是我救的他。”斯托特先生庄重地说，“我们斯托特家出身硬骨头的一族。我父亲就是个消防员——他从大火里救出过成千的人。”
这话他倒快说到点子上了，因为如前文所述，斯托特先生的父亲确实是位浸礼会牧师。

## XXXVI / 三十六

===Original===

“We must warn Miss Ardfern at once. I have been on the telephone with her this evening. I was enquiring about you, and the chances are that I so thoroughly alarmed her, that she is awake. I only hope to God she is,” said Carver.
But whilst it was easy earlier in the evening to get into touch with Hertford 906, it was now impossible. The Hertford operator, after the second attempt, signalled through that there was an interruption.
Carver came back to Mr. Stott’s dining-room with a grave face. They could speak without interruption because Mrs. Stott and the errant Eline had disappeared. Mr. Stott, his hands clasped across his stomach, was fast asleep in a chair, a touch of a smile on his lips. Probably he was dreaming of his heroic and hard bit’n ancestors.

===Chinese===

“我们必须立刻向阿德芬小姐示警。今晚我跟她在电话里谈过。我当时正打听你的下落，多半是我把她吓了个透，这会儿还醒着。我只求上帝保佑，她真的醒着。”卡弗说。
可是，傍晚时分要接通赫特福德 906 还轻而易举，此刻却无论如何也接不通了。赫特福德的接线生试到第二回，便传过话来，说线路中断了。
卡弗脸色凝重地回到斯托特先生的餐室。他们说话不必再有顾忌，因为斯托特太太和那个爱乱跑的艾琳已经不知去向。斯托特先生双手交叠搭在肚皮上，在椅子里睡得正香，嘴角挂着一丝笑意。多半他正梦见自家那些英勇顽强的硬骨头列祖列宗。

===Original===

“Tab,” said Carver, “you know Stone Cottage? Have you any recollection of the telephone arrangements? Is it a dead-end connection or is it connected from the road?”
“I think it is from the road,” said Tab, “the wire runs by the house and the connection crosses the garden. I remember because Ursula said how unsightly it was.”
Carver nodded.
“Then he’s there,” he said, “and the wire has been cut. I’ll get the nearest police station and see what we can do,” he said. “In the meantime we will find somebody with a car; make a few quick enquiries, Tab.”
Tab’s enquiries were particularly fortunate. In the very next house was a young man whose joy in life it was to exceed all speed limits on a sporting Spanz, and he accepted the commission which would enable him to break the laws with the approval of the police, with alacrity and enthusiasm.

===Chinese===

“塔布，”卡弗说，“石屋那边你熟吗？还记得电话线是怎么架的吗？是专线到底，还是从大路上分线接进去的？”
“我看是从大路上分进来的，”塔布说，“电线沿着宅子边上走，引入线横穿过花园。我记得清楚，因为乌苏拉说过那线有多难看。”
卡弗点了点头。
“那他人就在那儿了，”他说，“而且线已经被剪断了。我这就去联系最近的警察局，看看能想点什么办法，”他说，“这会儿咱们先找个有汽车的人；快去打听打听，塔布。”
塔布这一趟打听，运气好得出奇。隔壁那户人家正好住着个年轻人，此人生平最大乐事，就是驾着一辆运动型斯潘兹（Spanz）跑车把一切限速规矩踩在脚下。这趟差事能让他得了警察的批准去犯法，他欢天喜地、劲头十足地应承了下来。

===Original===

When Tab returned the Inspector was waiting at the garden gate.
“Is that the car?” he said. “Our friend knows the way?”
“I could find it blindfold,” said the amateur chauffeur.
It was a wild ride. Even Tab, who treated all speed regulations with scorn, admitted that the driver erred on the side of recklessness.
They spun through rain that stung and smarted like needles, that fell so fast that the two powerful lamps created fantastic nebulae and haloes in the darkness ahead. They skidded round greasy corners, thundered along narrow roads. Once Tab could have sworn he glimpsed a black car drawn up under a hedge. They passed before he could be sure.

===Chinese===

塔布回来时，探长已在花园门口等着了。
“就是这辆车？”他说，“咱们这位朋友认得路？”
“蒙上眼睛我也开得到。”那位业余司机说。
那真是一趟玩命的疾驰。就连把一切限速规矩视若无物的塔布，也承认这位司机未免鲁莽得过了头。
他们顶着大雨飞驰，雨点像针一样扎人脸面、火烧火燎地疼，下得又急，两盏大功率车灯在前头的黑暗里晃出光怪陆离的星云和光晕。他们在滑腻的弯道上打滑甩尾，在窄路上轰鸣疾驰。有一回塔布差点赌咒发誓，说自己瞥见一辆黑色汽车停在一道树篱下面。还没等他看清，车已经冲了过去。

===Original===

The garden gate was open when Tab leapt out from his precarious seat. As he came through the gate, a dangling wire struck him across the face.
There was no need to look for evidence of a visitor﻿—the door was open wide.
His heart was beating thunderously as he stood in the quiet hall, where the only sound that came to him was the sober ticking of a clock. He struck a match and lit one of the candles that he knew Ursula kept ready on a side table. By its faint light he saw that a chair in the hall had been overturned and lay on the carpet, which had been dragged up as though in a struggle. He held on to the wall for support.
“I’ll go alone,” he whispered hoarsely, and went up the stairs slowly. Every movement required an effort.
On the landing above a dim night light burnt. It was a broad landing, carpetted with a square blue carpet and there were two easy chairs and a small table-nest. Ursula had told him she sometimes read there, for there was a skylight overhead which could be opened on hot days. Here, again, the carpet was in disorder and on the blue settee﻿—

===Chinese===

塔布从那个险象环生的座位上跳下来时，花园门正敞着。他进门的一刹那，一根垂下来的电线抽在他脸上。
不必再寻找有人来过的痕迹了——房门大敞着。
他站在静悄悄的门厅里，心跳得像擂鼓，耳中唯一听得见的，是一座时钟不紧不慢的滴答声。他划了根火柴，点亮一支蜡烛——他知道乌苏拉总在一张边桌上备着。借着那点微光，他看见门厅里一把椅子翻倒在地，躺在地毯上，那地毯被抓掀得乱七八糟，仿佛经过了一场挣扎。他扶住墙壁撑住身子。
“我一个人上去。”他哑着嗓子低声说，随后一步一步慢慢上了楼梯。每一个动作都得攒上一把劲。
楼上楼梯平台点着一盏昏暗的夜灯。那是一片宽敞的平台，铺着一方蓝地毯，摆着两把安乐椅和一套嵌叠的小茶几。乌苏拉告诉过他，她有时在那里看书，因为头顶上有一扇天窗，天热的日子可以打开。这里，地毯同样乱作一团，而在那张蓝色长软椅上——

===Original===

He bit his lip to stop the cry that came.
Blood! A great patch near one end. He touched it frightfully, and looked at the tips of his fingers. Blood!
His knees gave way under him, and he sat down for a second, then with a tremendous effort, rose again and went to the door of Ursula’s room and turned the knob.
Shading the candle with his hands, he walked into the room. A figure was lying on the bed: the brown hair lay fan-like across the pillow, the face was turned away from him, and then﻿—His heart stood still.

===Chinese===

他咬住嘴唇，把冲到嗓子的喊声硬生生压了下去。
血！靠近一头有一大摊。他心惊胆战地伸手碰了碰，又看看指尖。血！
他双膝一软，颓然坐了一瞬，随即攒足一把大力气，重新站起来，走到乌苏拉的房门口，拧动了门把。
他用手拢着烛光，走进房里。床上躺着一个人影：一头棕发呈扇形铺在枕上，脸侧向一边背着他，随即——他的心骤然停止了跳动。

===Original===

“Who is that?” said a sleepy voice.
Ursula turned on her elbow, shading her eyes from the light of the candle.
“Ursula!” he breathed.
“Why﻿—it is Tab!”
He caught a glitter of steel as she thrust something back under the pillow, that she had half withdrawn.
“Tab!” She sat up in bed. “Why, Tab, what is wrong?”

===Chinese===

“是谁呀？”一个睡意惺忪的声音问。
乌苏拉撑着手肘侧过身来，抬手遮着蜡烛的光。
“乌苏拉！”他轻轻吁出一口气。
“咦——是塔布！”
她把一件抽出了一半的东西塞回枕头底下，他瞥见一道钢铁的微光。
“塔布！”她在床上坐起身来。“哎呀，塔布，出什么事了？”

===Original===

The candlestick was shaking in his hand, and he put it down on the table.
“What is wrong, dear?” she asked.
He could not answer; falling to his knees by the bedside, he trembled his relief into the crook of his arm.

===Chinese===

烛台在他手里直晃，他把它放到了桌上。
“怎么了，亲爱的？”她问。
他答不上来；双膝一屈跪倒在床边，把如释重负的颤抖，尽数埋进了臂弯里。

## XXXVII / 三十七

===Original===

Rex Lander was smiling as he drove through the rain, for it seemed to him that a great trouble had passed from his mind. The solution of all his difficulties had appeared miraculously. He did not hurry, the end was sure now and the woman who had completely occupied his mind for four years, whose portraits by the hundred he had secretly treasured, whose face he had watched, to whose voice he had listened night after night, until she had become an obsession that excluded all other thoughts and fancies, was his!
He had hated his sometime friend since the day Tab had made mock of his adoration. He had loathed him when the incredible fact had been proved beyond doubting, that Tab had stolen into the girl’s heart, and had won her in his absence.
He never doubted that with his great wealth, Ursula Ardfern was his wife for the asking. He had planned his life on this supposition. Wealth! The possession of great power, the ability to bestow upon the object of his choice all that human vanity or human weakness could desire.
Tab was dead now, he thought complacently, and his confession was ashes. He regretted the impulse which had made him write. He had had no intention of doing that when he brought Tab to Mayfield, and he was rather puzzled at his own stupidity. It was a mad thing to do. Mad? He frowned. He was not mad. It was very sane to desire a woman of Ursula Ardfern’s grace and beauty. It was sane enough to want money and to go to extremes to obtain what he wanted. Throughout all the ages men had killed others that their position might be enhanced. They were not madmen. And he was not mad. He had a definite plan, and madmen do not have definite plans.
Ursula would this night consent to marry him, and would be glad, if she refused, to consider her decision. He would be her accepted lover before he left the house, and the thought took his breath away.

===Chinese===

雷克斯·兰德驾车冒雨前行，脸上竟带着笑——在他看来，一桩天大的心事已然过去了。他一切难题的解法，就这么奇迹般地出现在眼前。他不慌不忙：如今结局已是板上钉钉，那个把他的心神整个占据了四年的女人——她成百幅的画像他一直偷偷珍藏，她的面容他凝望不倦，她的歌喉他一夜复一夜侧耳倾听，直到她成了一种排开其他一切思绪遐想的魔念——归他了！
打从塔布嘲弄他的倾慕的那一天起，他就恨上了这位昔日的朋友。等到那桩不可思议的事实被证明得再无可疑——塔布悄悄潜入了姑娘的心，趁他不在时赢得了她——他对塔布更是恨之入骨。
他从不怀疑：凭着万贯家财，乌苏拉·阿德芬只消他开口，便嫁他做妻子。他一辈子的盘算，就是照着这个设想铺排的。财富！那是握有莫大的权势，是有本事把自己看中的对象想要什么便给什么——世人虚荣也罢、软弱也罢，所能欲望的一切，无不可以赐予。
塔布如今死了，他洋洋自得地想，他的自白书也成了灰烬。他后悔那一时冲动，竟写下了那东西。他把塔布带到梅菲尔德去的时候，原本没有这个打算，如今想来，倒对自己的愚蠢有些纳闷。那是一件疯子才干得出来的事。疯了吗？他皱起眉头。他没疯。渴慕乌苏拉·阿德芬这般风姿与美貌的女人，清醒得很。想要钱、想得到想要的东西便不择手段，也清醒得很。古往今来，多少人杀掉旁人，好叫自己的地位更上一层。他们不是疯子。他也不是疯子。他有一套明确的计划，而疯子是没有明确的计划的。
乌苏拉今夜就会应允嫁他；倘若她拒绝了，也会乐意把她的决定重新掂量掂量。不等他离开这座宅子，他就会是她认可的情人了——这个念头激动得他气都透不过来。

===Original===

“Am I mad?” he asked aloud, as he parked his car in the side turning where Carver had almost found it once.
Madmen did not take such elaborate precautions. Madmen did not remember that by some mischance her servant might telephone for the police, nor carry in their pockets a weighted cord to throw over the telephone wire and bring it down. They did not even buy the cord of such and such a length, so much to bind Tab Holland, so much to break the wire and buy just sufficient for the purpose.
“I am not mad,” said Rex Lander, as he went in through the gate.
The house was in darkness; no lights glowed from the upper window where she was sleeping.

===Chinese===

“我疯了吗？”他把车停进那条岔路时，出声问道——卡弗有一回差点儿就在这儿发现了这辆车。
疯子不会做这般周密的防备。疯子不会记挂着万一有个闪失、她的仆人也许会打电话报警，也不会在口袋里揣一根坠了重物的绳索，预备抛上电话线把线拽下来。他们更不会把绳子买得这般长短有数——这么些捆塔布·荷兰，那么些拉断电线——分量掐得刚刚好，只够派这个用场。
“我没有疯。”雷克斯·兰德说着，走进了大门。
宅子一片漆黑；她睡着的楼上窗口，不透一丝灯光。

===Original===

He had made a very careful reconnaissance of the house, and knew its vulnerable points. He opened the casement window of the drawing-room, and had stepped softly inside the room before, in ordinary circumstances, a servant could have answered his ring at the door.
He was in her room! Her sitting-room! It held the very charm of her presence, and he would have been content to sit here, absorbing the atmosphere which she lent to everything she touched, dreaming dreams as he had dreamt so often in the night watches at Doughty Street, at his office, when he should have been working, in the solitary walk home from the theatre, after he had been listening entranced to her wonderful voice.
He took from his pocket a large electric torch and flashed it round. On the little cottage piano was a bowl of roses; reverently he drew one out, nipped off its stalk, and threaded it tenderly in his buttonhole. Her hand had placed it there. She had taken it from the garden, kissed it perhaps﻿—he bent his head, and his lips touched the velvety petals.
The door was not locked. He was in the hall, the wide-flagged hall. In the corner was a grandfather’s clock that ticked sedately.
Her room was in the front of the house: he knew he could not miss it, but must stand on the landing in an ecstasy of anticipation. He put down his torch upon the settee, took off his coat and mechanically smoothed his hair. Then he tiptoed forward. His hand was on the knob of the door when an arm came round his neck, a lithe sinewy arm that strangled the cry which rose in his throat.

===Chinese===

这座宅子他事先仔仔细细侦察过，知道它何处薄弱。他推开客厅的窗扉，轻手轻脚跨进屋里——寻常情形下，仆人还没来得及应门，他人早已到了屋里。
他进了她的房间！她的起居室！这屋里处处萦绕着她本人的韵致，他简直甘愿就在这里坐上一坐，浸在她赋予一切经手之物的气息里，做他的梦——在道蒂街深夜守更不寐的时分，在办公室里本该埋头工作的当口，在剧院散场后独自走回家的路上、听得神魂颠倒之后，他做过多少回这样的梦啊。
他从口袋里掏出一支大号电筒，朝四下里照了照。小型竖式钢琴上摆着一碗玫瑰；他毕恭毕敬地抽出一朵，掐去花茎，温柔地插进自己的钮孔。是她的手把它放在那儿的。她从园子里采下它，说不定还吻过它——他低下头，嘴唇碰上了那天鹅绒般的花瓣。
门没有上锁。他已在门厅里了——那铺着宽石板的门厅。角落里立着一座落地大摆钟，滴答、滴答，走得从容不迫。
她的房间在宅子正面：他知道绝不会找错，只消站上楼梯平台，在心醉神迷的期待里伫立片刻便了。他把电筒搁在长软椅上，脱下外套，又下意识地抿了抿头发。随后他踮起脚尖向前。他的手刚摸到门把，一条胳膊已从背后扼上他的脖颈——一条柔韧精瘦的胳膊，把他涌到喉头的喊声生生扼死了。

===Original===

Such was the man’s strength, that he lifted his assailant bodily from the ground, and twisting, would have flung him down, but Yeh Ling’s leg gripped his, and then Rex Lander wrenched his hands free and dived for his pocket. Yeh Ling saw the gleam of the automatic.
“Sorry,” he breathed.
It seemed to Rex Lander that he felt a momentary spasm of pain in his left side.
“You!” he gurgled. He coughed deeply once, and Yeh Ling eased him down to the settee.
The Chinaman stood, his head bent forward, listening. No sound but the *clac-cloc, clac-cloc* from the hall below. He lifted Lander’s eyelid’s and touched the ball of the eye gently. The man was dead.

===Chinese===

此人膂力竟有这般了得：他把偷袭自己的对手整个人凌空抱离了地面，一拧身就要将对方掼翻在地，可叶凌一条腿死死绞住了他的腿。雷克斯·兰德随即猛然挣脱双手，直往口袋里探。叶凌瞥见了自动手枪的寒光。
“对不住。”他低声道。
雷克斯·兰德只觉得左肋一阵短促的剧痛。
“是你！”他喉头咯咯地挤出一声。他沉沉咳了一下，叶凌把他轻轻放倒在长软椅上。
那中国人伫立不动，俯首倾听。除了楼下门厅里*嗒—嗒、嗒—嗒*的钟摆声，再无一丝动静。他掀起兰德的眼皮，轻轻碰了碰眼球。人已经死了。

===Original===

Yeh Ling pulled a blue silk handkerchief from his sleeve and wiped the perspiration from his face and eyes, replacing the handkerchief carefully. Then, bending down, he brought the limp arm of Lander about his neck, and with a jerk, lifted him to his shoulder. Slowly, painfully, he passed down the stairs with his burden. At the foot of the stairs he was compelled to lay the man down. He tried to find a chair, but without success. Sitting on the floor by the side of his victim, Yeh Ling recovered his breath, and getting up noiselessly, opened the door wide.
Black as the night was, there was sufficient light to distinguish objects faintly. He could not hoist the man again; he could only drag him across the hall. He knocked over a chair in the process, but fortunately it fell on the carpet and made no sound. Into the garden, along the paved path, out into the road.
Yeh Ling’s breath came in a thin whistle. He had to stop again to recover himself. He made another effort to lift the body and was partially successful. He staggered up the road, his knees giving way under him, but his will dominated, and when he reached a safe distance from the house, he put his burden down and went in search of Lander’s car. This he found with no trouble: it was unlikely that he should fail, for he had seen the man arrive. He started the engine and brought the car backward along the road until it was level with the Thing. Then he got down and hoisted it into the back of the car, and covering it with a coat he found, lit a cigarette, put on the lights, and drove slowly along the road toward Storford.

===Chinese===

叶凌从袖中抽出一方蓝绸手帕，拭去脸上眼上的汗水，又把手帕仔仔细细收回原处。随后他俯下身，将兰德那条绵软的胳膊拉上自己的肩颈，猛一发力，把人扛了起来。他驮着这副重担，一步一挨、艰难万状地下了楼。到了楼梯脚下，他不得不把人放下来。他想找把椅子，却没有找着。叶凌在死人身旁的地板上坐了坐，缓过一口气，便悄没声儿地起身，把大门敞开。
夜色尽管墨黑，也还有足够的微光把物影淡淡辨出。他再无力气把人重新扛起，只得拖着人横穿过门厅。半道碰翻了一把椅子，所幸倒在地毯上，没有出声。进了园子，沿着铺石小径，一直拖到大路上。
叶凌的呼吸细得像一线哨音。他不得不再停下来喘一喘。他又发一把力想把尸体抱起来，算是成了半截。他跌跌撞撞地沿路走去，两条膝弯直往下折，全凭一口气撑着；待到离宅子有了足够远的一段路，他放下担子，去找兰德的汽车。这一找毫不费事：找不着反倒是不可能——那人到场时，他原是亲眼看见的。他发动引擎，把车沿路倒回来，直到车身与那件东西并齐。随后他下了车，把它扛进车后座，拿车里寻着的一件外套盖上，点起一支香烟，开了车灯，沿着大路朝斯托福德缓缓驶去。

===Original===

Half-a-mile from where his new house was situated, he turned off the lights, and covered the remainder of the distance without their assistance. Drawing his car up close to a hedge, he gathered the limp figure on his shoulder, and tramped across the muddy ground until he came to the uprights that supported the cement vats. There was a flicker of lightning on the horizon. Yeh Ling could see in that flash (even if he had not known), that no progress had been made in the construction of his Pillar of Grateful Memories: the tub-like molds stood in place, the steel core, like an attenuated tree-trunk, leaned and swayed in the gale drunkenly.
After much seeking, he found the end of a rope fastened to one of the cross pieces of the platform, and this he tied about the Thing’s waist, and went to the windlass. A growl of thunder, a more prolonged quiver and splash of blue light. Looking up, Yeh Ling saw a bundle suspended in midair, and took another turn of the wheel.
The wind was blowing fiercely, sending that limp weight at the rope’s end swaying to and fro, and Yeh Ling peered up, striving to follow its every movement. Presently came another flash and another, and yet another. The body had swung over the edge of the mould. Yeh Ling released the primitive brake, and the body dropped. From his breast pocket he took the torch that he had found on the settee and flashed a light up at the wooden mould. Yes. The Thing had disappeared.

===Chinese===

离新宅还有半英里，他熄了车灯，摸黑走完剩下的路。他把车紧挨着一道树篱停妥，把那具绵软的身躯拢上肩头，蹚过泥泞的地面，一直走到支撑水泥槽模的那几根立柱跟前。天边闪起一道电光。就着那一闪（纵使他原本不知情，此刻也看清了），他的感戴记忆之柱毫无进展：桶状的模子原样立着，钢芯像一根细瘦的树干，在大风里醉汉似的倾侧摇晃。
寻了半晌，他找着了系在平台横木上的一根绳头，便把它捆在那件东西的腰间，然后走向绞盘。一声闷雷，一道更长的蓝光颤颤地泼洒下来。叶凌抬头一望，看见一团东西悬在半空，便又摇了一圈绞轮。
风刮得正紧，绳头那团绵软的重物被荡过来荡过去，叶凌仰着头眯眼细瞧，竭力盯住它的每一摆动。不多时又是一道闪光，又一道，再一道。尸身已经荡过了模槽的边沿。叶凌松开那简陋的制动闸，尸身便落了下去。他从胸前口袋里掏出那支在长软椅上捡到的电筒，朝木模槽里照了照。成了。那件东西不见了。

===Original===

There was a ladder against the wooden casing, and he climbed up, found another ladder inside, and descended the eight feet which intervened between the mould and the top of the hardening concrete beneath. Without loosening the rope, he dragged the body to its feet, and with quick, strong hands, lashed it to the steel core, winding the rope round and round. Presently he cut and knotted the binding, and climbed up again to the top of the woodwork, looking down in an effort to see the sagging figure. The lightning was now incessant, the thunder growing in intensity. He saw, and was satisfied. Pulling up the inside ladder, he dropped over, and in a few moments was himself back on ground level.
And now he made a search. He had to find the rope which controlled the shoot, and he discovered it at last. Pulling it gingerly, he heard the rush of the viscid concrete as it flowed down the shoot into the mould. He pulled the sluice gate wider yet, and heard the *swish, swish* of the flood as it gained in volume. After a while he released the rope, found a shovel, and climbed up the ladder again. The concrete had nearly reached the top of the mould. There was no sign of Rex Lander. Plying the tool, he levelled down the uneven surface of the cement, and descended for the last time.
The storm was local and passing, but if it had been the most cataclysmic disturbance of nature, Yeh Ling would not have noticed. He sat on the running-board of Lander’s car, wet to the skin, his hands raw and bleeding, every bone aching, and he smoked a cigarette and thought. So thinking, he heard the roar of an oncoming car, and ran to the cover of the hedge. It passed in a flash.

===Chinese===

木壳外靠着一架梯子，他攀了上去，见里面还有一架，便顺梯而下，一落八英尺，才从模口下到底下渐凝混凝土的顶面。他不解绳索，把尸身拽得直起身来，用一双迅捷有力的手，将它捆缚在钢芯上，绳子一圈又一圈地缠绕。缠罢，他割断余绳、打好收结，重新爬上木架顶端，低头张望，想看清那具瘫垂的人形。这时闪电已连绵不断，雷声一阵紧似一阵。他看清了，也满意了。他把里面那架梯子拽上去，翻身越出，片刻之间自己也落回了地面。
接着他开始搜寻。他必须找到控制溜槽的那根绳索，终于寻着了。他小心翼翼地一拉，听见黏稠的混凝土哗地一声涌下溜槽、灌进模槽。他把闸门又拉大了些，听那股浆流越涌越壮，*哗哗、哗哗*地响。过了一阵，他松开绳子，寻来一把铁锹，重新爬上梯子。混凝土差不多齐了模槽口。雷克斯·兰德已无半点踪影。他挥动铁锹，把水泥凹凸不平的表面整平，最后一次爬了下来。
这场雷雨只罩着一隅，转眼便将过去；可纵然是天崩地裂、乾坤倒转的大灾变，叶凌也不会有半分察觉。他坐在兰德汽车的踏脚板上，浑身湿透，双手磨得皮开血流，周身骨头无处不酸痛，一边抽着烟，一边想心事。正想着，他听见一辆汽车迎面驶来的轰响，便奔到树篱后藏身。那车一闪而过。

===Original===

“I cannot afford to wait,” said Yeh Ling.
He got into the car and drove off, avoiding Storford village, and taking instead, a road which led by the river. Here he stopped and got off, keeping his engines running. With his hands he released the clutch and the car tumbled down the bank into the black water. Then Yeh Ling went back for his own rattling machine.
When day was breaking, Yeh Ling lay in a hot scented bath in his apartment overlooking Reed Street. His hands, free from the water, held a thin selection of Browning’s poems; he was reading “Pippa Passes.”

===Chinese===

“我可等不起了。”叶凌说。
他上了车开走，避开斯托福德村，改取一条沿河的路。到了河边他停车下来，让引擎照旧转着。他徒手松开离合器，汽车便翻下河岸，栽进黑沉沉的水里。随后叶凌走回去，取他自己那辆哐啷作响的旧车。
天将破晓时分，叶凌躺在他俯瞰里德街的寓所里，泡在一缸热气腾腾、香馥氤氲的澡水中。他那双露出水面的手捧着一册薄薄的勃朗宁诗选；他正在读《皮帕走过》。

## XXXVIII / 三十八

===Original===

“There are bloodstains on the stairs,” said Carver, “and on the garden path outside. There is also the mark of car wheels which have evidently been backed from the lane where Lander usually kept his car, but beyond that, all trace is lost.”

He looked at Tab and Tab looked at him.

“What do you think?” asked Tab quietly.

“I am not putting my thoughts into words,” said the Inspector, “and I tell you honestly, Tab, that I’d rather have that confession of Lander’s﻿—wild and incoherent as it is﻿—than I’d have Lander himself.”

===Chinese===

“楼梯上有血迹，”卡弗说，“外面花园的小径上也有。还有车轮的印子，显然是从兰德平时停放汽车的那条小路上倒退出来的，可除此之外，一切踪迹都断了。

他望望塔布，塔布也望望他。

“你怎么看？”塔布轻声问。

“我不打算把心里想的说出口，”探长说，“而且我老实告诉你，塔布，兰德那份自白书——纵然疯疯癫癫、语无伦次——我宁可要它，也不要兰德本人。”


===Original===

Dawn was breaking, and Ursula had come down to make them coffee, a silent but absorbed listener.

“It is perfectly certain that Lander came here,” said Carver. “He destroyed the telephone connection, he made an entrance by the window in the sitting-room, and he went upstairs. You heard nothing, Miss Ardfern?”

“Nothing.” She shook her head. “I am not a very light sleeper, but I am sure if there had been any kind of struggle outside my door, I should have heard.”

“It all depends on who controlled the struggle,” said Carver drily. “My own belief is﻿—however, that is nothing to do with the matter. There is the fact that Lander’s hat was found in the roadway, Lander came here, his car marks are distinguishable, and that Lander himself has gone. Turner heard nothing?”

“Nothing,” she said. “That isn’t remarkable; he sleeps at the back of the house, in a room opening from the kitchen. Does the confession tell you much?”

===Chinese===

天正破晓，乌苏拉下楼来给他们煮咖啡；她一言不发，却听得入神。

“兰德来过这里，这是千真万确的，”卡弗说。“他弄断了电话线，从客厅的窗户进屋，随后上了楼。您什么也没听见吗，阿德芬小姐？”

“什么也没听见。”她摇了摇头。“我睡觉不算惊醒，可我敢肯定，倘若我门外发生过任何动静的挣扎，我一定听得见。”

“那全看那场搏斗由谁掌控着。”卡弗冷冷地说。“我个人的看法是——不过，这与本案无关。事实摆在这里：兰德的帽子在马路上被找到，兰德来过这里，他的车辙清晰可辨，而兰德本人已经走了。特纳什么也没听见？”

“什么也没有。”她说。“这没什么可怪的；他睡在房子后部，屋子从厨房进去。那份自白书能告诉你们很多吗？”


===Original===

“A whole lot,” said Carver emphatically, “and with Tab’s explanation as to how the key was put back on the table, the thing is as clear as daylight. It seems that Lander has for years been planning to get his uncle’s money, and his scheme was hurried when he learnt﻿—probably from the old man’s lips when he was staying with him﻿—that Trasmere intended leaving his money away from the family. Whilst Rex Lander was a guest at Mayfield, he must have taken the revolver which was undoubtedly Trasmere’s property, and I have an idea that he took something else.”

“I can tell you what it was,” said Ursula quietly. “He took away with him some Mayfield notepaper.”

===Chinese===

“能告诉你们一大堆，”卡弗语气斩钉截铁，“再加上塔布讲明了那把钥匙是怎样回到桌上的，整件事已如白昼般清楚。看来，兰德谋算他叔父的钱财已有多年，而当他得知——多半是借住老人那里时从老人口中亲耳听来的——特拉斯米尔打算把钱财遗到家族之外，他的计划便加快了步伐。雷克斯·兰德在梅菲尔德做客期间，必定拿走了那把左轮手枪——那无疑是特拉斯米尔之物——而且我料想，他还拿走了另一样东西。”

“我能告诉你们那是什么，”乌苏拉轻声说。“他随身带走了一些梅菲尔德的信纸。”


===Original===

Tab looked at her in astonishment.

“Why should he do that, Ursula?”

She did not answer him at once, because here Carver interposed a question.

“How long have you known, Miss Ardfern, that Lander was the murderer of Jesse Trasmere?”

===Chinese===

塔布愕然望着她。

“他要那个做什么，乌苏拉？”

她没有立即回答他，因为卡弗在这时插进来一个问题。

“阿德芬小姐，您知道兰德是杀害杰西·特拉斯米尔的凶手，有多久了？”


===Original===

Tab expected her to say that she did not know it at all, and that the news had come in the nature of a shock. Instead:

“I knew he was the murderer the day that Tab told me about the will Mr. Trasmere had left.”

“But why?” asked Tab.

“Because,” said Ursula, “Mr. Trasmere could not read or write English!”

===Chinese===

塔布本以为她会说自己根本不知情，这消息于她无异于一场惊骇。谁知她的回答是：

“塔布跟我说起特拉斯米尔先生留下遗嘱的那一天，我就知道他是凶手了。”

“那是为什么？”塔布问。

“因为，”乌苏拉说，“特拉斯米尔先生根本不会读、不会写英文！”


===Original===

The full significance of the simple statement came more quickly to Carver than to Tab.

“I see. I’ve known the will was a fake all the time,” he said, “but I thought it was just a forgery, and that Lander had imitated the writing of the letters that used to come for him from the old man.”

“They never came from the old man, Mr. Lander wrote them himself,” said the girl. “I rather think he wrote them with the intention of establishing the authenticity of the signature when the will was discovered. He had guessed the old gentleman’s secret. Mr. Trasmere was very sensitive on the point. He used to complain that although he could write and read Chinese without any difficulty﻿—in fact, I have learnt since that he was scholarly in that direction﻿—he could not write two words of English. That is the principal explanation as to why he employed me for his secretary, and why he must have somebody upon whom he could place the utmost reliance, and on whom he had some sort of pull.”

===Chinese===

这句平平淡淡的话所含的全部深意，卡弗比塔布领会得更快。

“我明白了。那份遗嘱是假的，这一点我从头就知道，”他说，“不过我一直以为那不过是一桩伪造，是兰德模仿了往常老人寄给他的那些信件的笔迹。”

“那些信从来不是出自老人之手，全是兰德先生自己写的，”姑娘说。“我倒认为，他写那些信本就是存了用心的：待到遗嘱被发现之日，好坐实那个签名的真确。他早已猜中老先生的秘密。特拉斯米尔先生对这一点极为敏感。他从前常常抱怨，说自己读写中文毫无困难——事实上，我后来才知道，他在这上头造诣颇深，称得上有学问——可英文却连两个字也写不出来。这也就是他为何雇我做秘书、为何他身边必须有一个既能托付以极致信任、而他又多少能拿捏得住的人的主要缘故。”


===Original===

“Do you mean to tell me that Rex was writing letters to himself?” asked Tab incredulously.

She nodded.

“There is no doubt at all,” she said. “When you told me Mr. Trasmere had left a will in his own handwriting, I nearly fainted. I knew then just what had happened, who was the murderer and why Mr. Trasmere had been murdered.”

Carver rubbed his unshaven chin.

===Chinese===

“你是要告诉我，那些信都是雷克斯写给自己的？”塔布难以置信地问。

她点了点头。

“这一点毫无疑问，”她说。“你告诉我特拉斯米尔先生留下一份亲笔遗嘱时，我差点儿晕过去。那一刻我就全明白了：出了什么事，凶手是谁，特拉斯米尔先生又为什么被杀。”

卡弗摩挲着自己没刮过的下巴。


===Original===

“I wish I could find Lander,” he said half to himself.

“How long did Rex have this idea?”

Tab broke the silence which followed.

“For years, ever since﻿—” he hesitated.

“Ever since he first saw me?” said the girl miserably.

===Chinese===

“但愿我能找到兰德。”他半是自言自语地说。

“雷克斯存这个念头有多久了？”

塔布打破了随之而来的沉默。

“好多年了，打从——”他迟疑了。

“打从他头一回见我？”姑娘凄然说。


===Original===

“Before then. There was another lady upon whom he set his heart,” replied Carver. “Lander, as I say, had to hurry up his scheme when he found that the money was going to be left away. He was only waiting his opportunity. The plan had been completed to the smallest detail. He had practiced with the key trick assiduously, and he decided to put the plan into operation on the day the murder was committed. He knew that his uncle generally spent his Saturday afternoons in the vault, that the doors leading to the vault would be open. His first job was to get rid of the servant. By some means he discovered that Walters was a crook: I have an idea that there was a time when Lander was an industrious student of crime, and I seem to remember somebody telling me that he used to spend hours at the *Megaphone* library and made himself very unpopular in consequence.”

Tab nodded.

===Chinese===

“比那还早。他还倾心过另一位女士呢。”卡弗答道。“我方才说过，兰德发现那笔钱就要外流，只得加紧催动他的计划。他等的就是一个机会。整个方案早已细化到最末一节。他孜孜不倦地练熟了那套钥匙把戏，并决定在案发当天将计划付诸实施。他知道他叔父的星期六下午照例是在保险库里过的，通向保险库的几道门都会敞开。他的头一桩事就是把仆人打发走。他不知通过什么途径查出沃尔特斯是个惯犯：我总觉得，兰德有一阵子曾是犯罪这门学问的勤勉学生，而且我仿佛记得有谁对我说过，他常在《扩音器报》的资料室里一泡就是好几个钟头，也因此弄得人人都嫌恶他。”

塔布点了点头。


===Original===

“That is where he might have become acquainted with Walters, or Felling, though I am not going to dogmatise on the subject. It is sufficient that he found that Walters was a convicted thief, and that on the afternoon of the murder he sent a telegram (which I have been able to trace) to Walters, telling him the police were coming for him at three. From the moment he saw that telegram delivered, and he must have been watching, to the moment that Walters left the house, Lander was somewhere handy. As soon as he saw the door opened, and Walters came out, he made his appearance. When Walters had gone, he went into the house, passed down the steps into the passage, and found, as he had expected, his uncle working at the table, probably checking some money that had come in during the week﻿—a favourite occupation of his. Without warning he shot the old man dead. Then, looking round for the key, he found that it was not, as he had expected, in the lock, but on the chain about Trasmere’s neck. He broke the chain and took out the key, which was bloodstained. He had a pin and thread ready which he fastened to the centre of the table, put the other end through the keyhole after threading the key, pulled the door to, locked it, and drew on the slack in exactly the same way as you saw and described, Tab.

===Chinese===

“他结识沃尔特斯——或者说费林——也许正是在那儿，不过这个题目我不愿妄下断语。要紧的是：他查明了沃尔特斯是个判过刑的窃贼，而且在案发当天下午，他给沃尔特斯发了一封电报（这封电报我已查到了），告诉他警察三点要来抓他。从眼见那封电报送达的那一刻起——他必定一直在暗中盯着——到沃尔特斯离开宅子的那一刻止，兰德始终守在近旁的什么地方。一见门开、沃尔特斯出来，他便现身了。等沃尔特斯走远，他进了宅子，顺台阶而下走进过道，果然不出所料，他叔父正在桌前忙活，多半是在清点这个星期进账的款项——那是老人的一大癖好。他毫无预兆地一枪打死了老人。随后他四下寻找钥匙，却发现钥匙并不像他预想的那样插在锁上，而是挂在特拉斯米尔脖子的链子上。他扯断链子，取下钥匙，钥匙上染着血。他早已备好一枚别针和一条棉线，把线固定在桌子中央，将钥匙穿上线，再把线的另一头从锁眼里递出去，随即带上门，落了锁，收紧松余的线——手法与你亲眼所见、亲口描述的分毫不差，塔布。


===Original===

“I noticed one little bloodstain near the bottom of the door when I first inspected the cell, but could not make head or tail of it. Nor could I understand the appearance of a tiny piece of grit in the ward of the key. Both these mysteries have been solved. When the key was back on the table, he pulled out the pin, removed it from the cotton, which he put back in his pocket, and by some mischance, dropped the pin in the passageway.”

There was another long pause, and then:

===Chinese===

“我头一回勘查那间保险库时，便注意到门脚下有一小点血迹，却怎么也参不透。钥匙锁槽里嵌着一粒细砂，我也同样莫名其妙。如今这两个谜都已解开。钥匙回到桌上之后，他把别针拽了出来，从棉线上解下别针，棉线收回口袋，却不料一时失手，把别针掉在了过道里。”

又是一阵长长的静默，然后：


===Original===

“Where is he now?” asked Carver irritably.

The only man who could have supplied him with exact information was at that moment sleeping peacefully on a hard and narrow bed.

===Chinese===

“他现在人在哪儿？”卡弗烦躁地问。

唯一能向他提供确切消息的那个人，此刻正在一张又硬又窄的床上睡得香甜安稳。
